  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that when participants in a public goods game with punishment are competing to be chosen for a subsequent trust game, antisocial punishment (punishing high contributors) is much higher than in a standard PGG with punishment. This effect is present from the first round and persists across rounds. In the control condition, contributions increase over rounds (as is typical when punishment is available), but in the markets condition, contributions do not increase, suggesting that antisocial punishment suppresses the escalation of cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral pattern implies that the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment may be undermined or reversed in contexts with high antisocial punishment driven by partner competition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the context of partner competition (biological markets) can substantially increase antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, which in turn suppresses the usual increase in cooperation seen with punishment. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the findings imply that in environments where partner choice or competitive altruism is salient, enabling punishment may not improve and may even harm group efficiency, as antisocial punishment undermines cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of partner competition or similar social comparison incentives is a critical moderator: in such settings, the efficiency benefit of punishment is likely reduced or reversed, even if control efficiency is known. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Grossman, G., & Baldassarri, D. (2012). The Impact of Elections on Cooperation: Evidence from a Lab-in-the-Field Experiment in Uganda. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The introduction of a centralized sanctioning authority (monitor) in a PGG increases contributions to the public good compared to a baseline with no punishment. When the monitor is elected rather than randomly assigned, contributions increase further, suggesting a legitimacy effect. Both types of monitors punish defectors at a personal cost, but the actual punishment strategies are similar between elected and random monitors. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The findings are robust to controls for monitor characteristics and are supported by observational data linking legitimacy to cooperation in real-world farmer groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing centralized punishment in a PGG increases contributions, and that the legitimacy of the punisher (via election) further increases cooperation. However, because the main outcome is contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the experimental manipulation is clear, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff reporting limits the precision of predictions for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Jin, S. X., Columbus, S., van Lange, P. A. M., & Balliet, D. (2024). Conflict, cooperation, and institutional choice. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two experiments, higher conflict of interests (lower MPCR) leads to lower cooperation, lower beliefs about others' cooperation, and lower normative expectations and personal norms of cooperation. However, the degree of conflict does not increase the likelihood that individuals or groups vote to establish a sanctioning institution (peer punishment, centralized punishment, or gossip/ostracism). Centralized punishment is more likely to be chosen than peer punishment or gossip/ostracism, but this is not moderated by conflict level. The main outcomes are behavioral (contributions, beliefs, norms, votes), not efficiency or group payoff. There is no direct evidence on how enabling punishment affects efficiency or group payoff relative to a no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how conflict of interests and institutional choice affect cooperation, beliefs, and norms in public goods games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment. Thus, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does show that the mere availability of punishment institutions (when endogenously chosen) is not more likely under high conflict, and that cooperation declines more rapidly under high conflict. The lack of efficiency or payoff analysis means this paper should be used for context and mechanism, not for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). Undermining trust and cooperation: The paradox of sanctioning systems in social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that sanctioning systems in public goods games can undermine trust in others' intrinsic motivation to cooperate. When the sanctioning system is removed, both trust and cooperation decrease, especially among participants with high initial trust. The negative effect on cooperation is mediated by the reduction in trust. The effect is stronger for large sanctions and when initial trust is high. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment system can undermine trust and, after its removal, reduce cooperation, especially when initial trust is high. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on baseline trust levels and that the removal of punishment can have negative aftereffects on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and potential moderators (such as initial trust and punishment size), rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Krupp, D. B., Debruine, L. A., & Barclay, P. (2008). A cue of kinship promotes cooperation for the public good. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that subtle cues of kinship (facial self-resemblance) increase cooperation in a public goods game with punishment. Participants contributed more to the public good as the number of self-resembling (kin-cue) group members increased, but did not change their punishment behavior toward free riders based on kinship cues. The experiment used a yoked control design to isolate the effect of kinship cues. The main outcomes are contribution and punishment levels; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured. The findings suggest that kinship cues can promote cooperation, but do not alter punitive responses to free riding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a standard public goods game with punishment, increasing perceived kinship among group members increases contributions but does not affect punishment rates. While efficiency is not directly measured, higher contributions generally imply higher efficiency, unless offset by excessive punishment costs. Since punishment rates did not increase, it is reasonable to infer that efficiency would likely increase with kinship cues. However, the paper does not manipulate or analyze any of the core game design dimensions (such as MPCR, punishment cost, or player count) except for the kinship cue. Thus, its direct value for predicting efficiency as a function of game design dimensions is limited, but it does suggest that social context (kinship cues) can moderate cooperation independently of punishment mechanics.

- source: Xu, Z. C. (2023). Obedience to the symbol of authority: Experimental evidence on the symbolic source of legitimate authority*. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing a symbolic scepter for the authority significantly increases citizens' support for the authority's punishment decisions, regardless of the payment scheme (appropriation vs. fixed salary) or information transparency. However, the symbol does not affect the frequency or style of punishment by authorities, nor does it affect citizens' contribution rates or group/individual payoffs. The mechanism is psychological: the symbol increases intrinsic obedience and perceived legitimacy, not actual cooperation or efficiency. There is no evidence that the symbol improves efficiency, welfare, or total group earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that symbolic manipulations of authority can increase perceived legitimacy (support for punishment) in public goods games with punishment, but do not affect efficiency, group payoffs, or contribution rates. For the downstream prediction task, the presence or absence of a symbolic authority is unlikely to affect the efficiency outcome when punishment is enabled, holding other game design dimensions and control efficiency constant. The main moderators (payment scheme, transparency) do not interact with the symbol to affect efficiency. Thus, symbolic authority should not be expected to improve efficiency in similar lab PGG environments.

- source: Chen, F. D., Nave, G., & Wang, L. (2025). Calculated Punishment. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both prosocial and antisocial punishment rates are strongly affected by the cost and impact of punishment. When punishment is free, antisocial punishment increases substantially. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but the findings imply that the design of punishment (especially cost) can influence the prevalence of antisocial punishment, which is typically detrimental to group welfare. The main contribution is a process-level account of punishment decisions, showing that people are not intuitively predisposed to punish, and that punishment is a calculated response sensitive to incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the cost and impact (effectiveness) of punishment are critical moderators of punishment behavior in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the finding that antisocial punishment increases when punishment is free suggests that low-cost punishment can undermine group welfare. For prediction tasks, the results support the importance of including punishment cost and effectiveness as key dimensions, and caution that enabling low-cost punishment may not always improve efficiency due to increased antisocial punishment. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so its value is mainly in highlighting moderators and mechanisms.

- source: Lubell, M., & Scholz, J. T. (2001). Cooperation, reciprocity, and the collective-action heuristic. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that both reciprocity and niceness in the environment increase cooperation rates, but their effects depend on whether subjects are initially predisposed to cooperate or defect. Past experience with reciprocity increases cooperation even when current reciprocity is absent, while past nonreciprocity does not reduce cooperation when reciprocity is present. The introduction of punishment (penalties) increases cooperation among defectors and in nonreciprocal environments, but can reduce cooperation among initial cooperators in reciprocal environments. The effect of punishment is thus asymmetric: it is most beneficial in environments lacking reciprocity, but may undermine cooperation where social capital is already high. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not direct efficiency or group payoff, but the authors discuss the implications for the evolution of cooperation and social capital.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends on the underlying level of reciprocity and the initial disposition of players. Punishment increases cooperation in environments where reciprocity is low (nonreciprocal), but can reduce cooperation among those already inclined to cooperate in high-reciprocity environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive in low-cooperation, low-reciprocity settings, but may be neutral or even negative in high-cooperation, high-reciprocity settings. However, since the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, these implications are inferred from cooperation rates. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are well specified, but the main outcome is behavioral (cooperation), not payoff-based.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Punishment, and Cooperation Across 18 Societies: A Meta-Analysis. *Perspectives on Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase cooperation in public goods games, with a moderate average effect (d=0.77, 95% CI [0.63, 0.91]), but with substantial cross-societal variation. The effect of punishment is significantly stronger in high-trust societies. Methodological moderators show that punishment is more effective in partner-matching protocols (fixed groups) and with more iterations (rounds), but not significantly affected by group size. Other societal variables (GDP, democracy, market competition, religious participation, norms of civic cooperation) have weaker or non-significant effects after controlling for trust. The outcome measured is behavioral cooperation/contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the standardized Fehr & Gächter public goods game, enabling peer punishment increases cooperation rates, especially in high-trust societies. However, the outcome is contribution/cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone. The findings suggest that, for lab PGGs with 3-7 players, 6-50 rounds, and standard costly peer punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MUs), punishment increases cooperation, with larger effects in partner-matching and longer games. The effect on efficiency is likely positive if higher cooperation translates to higher group payoffs, but this is not directly measured. The paper is highly informative for predicting behavioral cooperation under different game designs and societal contexts, but less so for direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., Zettler, I., & Heydasch, T. (2012). Personality, Punishment and Public Goods: Strategic Shifts Towards Cooperation as a Matter of Dispositional Honesty-Humility. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The presence of punishment in a PGG increases individual contributions to the public good, replicating prior findings. This effect is moderated by the Honesty-Humility personality trait: individuals low in Honesty-Humility increase their contributions substantially when punishment is possible, while those high in Honesty-Humility contribute at a high level regardless of punishment. The study uses hypothetical (non-incentivized) PGGs, and the main outcome is contribution behavior, not actual efficiency or group payoff. A simulation based on observed contributions suggests that group efficiency (total group gain) increases with the number of high Honesty-Humility members, but this is not an experimental outcome. The findings are robust across two experiments, including one with a large, heterogeneous sample and a substantial time lag between personality and PGG measurement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment increases contributions in PGGs, especially among individuals low in Honesty-Humility. However, because the experiments are hypothetical and do not use real monetary incentives, and because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured as an experimental outcome, the findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates rather than efficiency per se. The simulation of group efficiency based on observed contributions provides indirect evidence that punishment could increase efficiency, but this is not a direct experimental result. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers informative indirect evidence that punishment increases contributions (and thus likely efficiency), but the lack of real incentives and direct efficiency measurement are important limitations.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2016). Exploring an Effective Incentive System on a Groupware. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of public-goods-like meta-sanction games, systems with first-order punishment and appropriate second-order sanctions (such as punishing non-punishers or rewarding punishers) can achieve and robustly sustain high cooperation rates. In contrast, systems that allow first-order rewards without second-order incentives for rewarding behavior can actually impede the formation of cooperative regimes. The cooperation rate is highly sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio of sanctions and the discount factor for higher-order sanctions. The results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the structure of the punishment and reward system (including the presence or absence of second-order incentives) strongly affects the cooperation rate in public-goods-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (with appropriate cost/benefit ratios and possibly second-order sanctions) can increase cooperation rates, but the translation to efficiency is not quantified. The results also warn that adding rewards without second-order incentives can reduce cooperation, which may imply lower efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Baldassarri, D. (2015). Cooperative Networks: Altruism, Group Solidarity, Reciprocity, and Sanctioning in Ugandan Producer Organizations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both communication and the threat of sanctioning increase contributions in a repeated PGG compared to baseline. Communication (reciprocity) has a stronger effect than punishment. However, only cooperation in the communication condition predicts real-life cooperative behaviors in the farmer organizations. The threat of sanctioning increases contributions in the lab, but this does not translate to real-world cooperation among council members. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in small, cohesive groups with repeated interaction, communication (reciprocity) is a more effective and relevant mechanism for promoting cooperation than punishment. While punishment increases contributions in the lab PGG, it does not predict real-world cooperative behavior in this context. The absence of efficiency or payoff data means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it suggests that in similar settings, punishment may not translate into real-world cooperative gains. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that communication/reciprocity is a key moderator, and that punishment's effect may be context-dependent and less robust in small, high-trust groups.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Lun, J. (2015). Societal threat and cultural variation in the strength of social norms: An evolutionary basis. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the PGG with punishment, higher societal threat leads to higher rates of cooperation and a greater proportion of norm-enforcing punishers. Under low threat, cooperation and norm enforcement are weaker. The presence of punishment is necessary to achieve high cooperation under threat; spatial structure alone is insufficient. The model also shows that norm strength (adherence and enforcement) increases rapidly in response to increased threat and declines gradually when threat is removed. While payoffs are modeled, the main reported outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement in PGG-like environments, especially under higher threat. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment enabled/disabled, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The model parameters (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) are specified, and the model is robust to parameter changes, but the main outcomes are behavioral. The paper supports the qualitative prediction that enabling punishment increases cooperation and norm enforcement, which is likely to increase efficiency, but does not provide direct efficiency data for prediction tasks.

- source: Van Vugt, M., & De Cremer, D. (1999). Leadership in social dilemmas: The effects of group identification on collective actions to provide public goods. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Instrumental (punishing) leaders increase contributions to the public good, especially when group identification is low. (2) Relational (encouraging) leaders only increase contributions when group identification is high. (3) In low group identification groups, relational leaders are ineffective and may even be counterproductive. (4) The legitimacy of the leader mediates the effect on contributions. (5) There is a general preference for democratic, elected, and internal leaders, especially when group identification is high. (6) No direct group payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, but the pattern of contribution changes is clear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment (via an instrumental leader) in increasing contributions depends on group identification. In low group identification settings, punishment is much more effective than encouragement. In high group identification settings, both punishment and encouragement can be effective. However, the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are partially specified and can inform dimension coding, but the main outcome is contribution, not efficiency.

- source: Lindström, B., Jangard, S., Selbing, I., & Olsson, A. (2018). The Role of a Common Is Moral Heuristic in the Stability and Change of Moral Norms. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across nine experiments, participants observing one-shot PGGs judged common behaviors (whether altruistic or selfish) as more moral and less deserving of punishment than rare behaviors. This effect was robust across lab and online samples, and held for both preexisting moral norms (cooperation/selfishness) and arbitrary behaviors. In one experiment, participants could spend real money to punish, and commonness reduced costly punishment of selfishness. The effect of commonness was explained by a formal model of social influence (Social Impact Theory). Agent-based simulations showed that the CIM heuristic can generate both stability and sudden change in group-level moral norms. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived commonness of behaviors in PGGs shapes moral and punishment judgments, and that common selfishness is punished less than rare selfishness. It also shows that costly punishment is reduced when selfishness is common. However, because the experiments are one-shot and do not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, the findings do not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The results are most useful for understanding psychological and behavioral responses to norm violation and punishment, rather than for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions in repeated or strategic PGGs.

- source: Stagnaro, M. N., Arechar, A. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). From good institutions to generous citizens: Top-down incentives to cooperate promote subsequent prosociality but not norm enforcement. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG (with 3 players, 10 rounds, and varying inspection probabilities) leads to a substantial increase in contribution rates compared to a no-punishment control. This institutional exposure also increases subsequent prosociality in a Dictator Game, but does not directly increase punishment behavior in a Third-Party Punishment Game. The effect on punishment is only indirect, mediated by increased prosociality. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a centralized punishment institution in a repeated PGG increases contribution rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly. The design dimensions (player count, number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, and the manipulation of punishment is clear. However, for the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper only provides indirect support via contribution rates. It does not allow direct estimation of efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that even minimal punishment is sufficient to substantially increase cooperation, and that further increases in punishment probability do not yield additional gains in contribution. The absence of direct efficiency data is a limitation for prediction.

- source: Odouard, V. V., Smirnova, D., & Edelman, S. (2023). Polarize, catalyze, and stabilize: how a minority of norm internalizers amplify group selection and punishment. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of norm internalizers in the strategy space (even as a small minority, around 10% of the population) leads to higher mean cooperation rates, longer and more stable periods of high cooperation, and fewer long periods of low cooperation, compared to conditions with only group selection and punishment. This effect is most pronounced at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios, where cooperation is possible but not guaranteed. The mechanism is indirect: norm internalizers polarize groups (increasing inter-group variance), catalyze spikes in cooperation when global cooperation is low, and stabilize high-cooperation states. The effect is not due to norm internalizers themselves cooperating more, but to their influence on the behavior of other agents. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of public goods games with group selection and imperfect punishment, adding a minority of norm internalizers can substantially increase mean cooperation rates, especially at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency: higher cooperation rates likely imply higher efficiency, but the mapping is not quantified here. The model assumes punishment is imperfect (not all defections are observed), and punishment is implemented as withholding benefits rather than costly punishment. The results are robust across two different model structures (abstract and spatial/naturalistic). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of norm internalization mechanisms can amplify the effect of punishment and group selection on cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency.

- source: Jaffe, K., & Zaballa, L. (2010). Co-Operative Punishment Cements Social Cohesion. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that co-operative punishment (where the cost of punishing free-riders is shared among group members) is much more effective at maintaining a high proportion of contributors in a simulated public goods game than either no punishment or altruistic punishment (where punishers pay the cost individually). The effectiveness of punishment depends on the ratio of cost to punished versus cost to punisher, and on the efficiency of detecting free-riders. Co-operative punishment allows pro-social behavior to invade and persist in populations of free-riders, while altruistic punishment does not. However, the primary outcome is the proportion of contributors, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the structure of punishment (co-operative vs. individual) is a critical moderator of cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, the maintenance of high contribution rates under co-operative punishment suggests that efficiency would likely be higher in such settings, especially when the cost of punishment is shared and detection of free-riders is effective. For prediction tasks, the results imply that enabling co-operative punishment mechanisms (where costs are distributed) should be expected to yield higher efficiency than individual punishment, all else equal. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Balliet, D., Mulder, L. B., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2011). Reward, Punishment, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, punishment_cost, reward_cost
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that both punishment and reward robustly increase cooperation in public goods and related social dilemma games. The effect of punishment is stronger when it is costly to the punisher and when participants interact repeatedly with the same group (partner matching). Decentralized punishment is more effective than centralized punishment, but this is largely explained by the correlation with costliness. Group size and cost-to-fine ratio do not significantly moderate the effect. While the main outcomes are cooperation rates, the paper discusses that punishment can reduce group efficiency in the short run (due to punishment costs) but may increase efficiency in the long run as cooperation stabilizes and punishment use declines. The effect of punishment varies by country, with some countries (e.g., Russia) showing no positive effect. The paper does not provide meta-analytic estimates of efficiency or group payoff, but references to primary studies suggest that efficiency effects are context-dependent and may be negative in the short term and positive in the long term.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods and similar social dilemma games, enabling punishment increases cooperation rates, especially when punishment is costly and the game is repeated with fixed partners. However, the paper does not provide direct meta-analytic estimates of efficiency or group payoff, which are the target outcomes for the prediction task. The discussion and references to primary studies suggest that efficiency may initially decrease when punishment is introduced (due to punishment costs), but can increase over time as cooperation rises and punishment use declines. For prediction, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be more positive in longer, repeated games with partner matching and costly punishment, but may be less positive or even negative in short or one-shot games, or when punishment is free or centralized. The lack of direct efficiency outcomes in the meta-analysis limits the precision of quantitative prediction for efficiency, but the qualitative patterns are informative for understanding moderators.

  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that group-level convergence in cooperation (as measured by ICC) increases over the first five rounds of repeated PGGs and plateaus between 20% and 50% under standard conditions. The presence of punishment opportunities and detailed feedback (showing all group members' decisions) substantially increases group convergence, sometimes up to 70%. Small groups (≤4 players) and groups with homogenous social preferences also show higher convergence. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment opportunities and detailed feedback increase group-level convergence in cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially in small and homogenous groups. However, because the primary outcome is ICC (convergence in contributions) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is likely to increase behavioral alignment within groups, which may be associated with higher or lower efficiency depending on the equilibrium reached, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: van Dijk, E., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Experimental Games and Social Decision Making. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 72*.

  findings: The review highlights that the inclusion of punishment and reward mechanisms in repeated public goods games and other social dilemmas enables partner control, which can promote cooperation and the development of group norms. It notes that punishment is typically costly to the punisher but more costly to the target, and that such mechanisms can shape future cooperation through reputation and indirect reciprocity. The review also discusses moderators such as group heterogeneity, intergroup competition, and cross-cultural variation in norm enforcement. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative synthesis on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.

It identifies key design features (e.g., repeated play, sanctioning options, partner selection) that are relevant to predicting the impact of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to calibrate the magnitude of punishment's effect on efficiency. Its value for prediction is mainly in identifying which game dimensions are theoretically important moderators.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schurtenberger, I. (2018). Normative foundations of human cooperation. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social norms, particularly conditional cooperation norms, are central to explaining cooperation in public goods games. Punishment opportunities increase cooperation rates, but the welfare (efficiency) effects are often limited or negative due to the costs of punishment and the occurrence of antisocial punishment. Normative constraints on punishment—such as those enabled by institutional or group-level norm formation—can reduce antisocial punishment and make punishment more effective, leading to higher cooperation with less collateral cost. The review emphasizes that the presence of punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the structure and normative context of punishment matter greatly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and integrative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff unless punishment is normatively constrained to reduce antisocial punishment and collateral costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of normative or institutional constraints, the prevalence of antisocial punishment, and the cultural context. Game design dimensions that affect the cost, targeting, and normative context of punishment are likely to be critical moderators of efficiency outcomes. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes, but it cautions against assuming that increased cooperation from punishment always translates into higher efficiency.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Social norms and human cooperation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the opportunity to punish norm violators in public goods games leads to a dramatic increase in cooperation rates, often approaching full cooperation. Punishment is frequently administered by group members or even third parties, and is motivated by non-selfish concerns for norm enforcement rather than self-interest. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency and strength, but punishment remains prevalent even when it is costly. The presence of punishment opportunities transforms the dynamics of cooperation, preventing the typical decline in contributions over time seen in standard public goods games without punishment. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation rates, and that the effect is robust across various experimental designs. For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment will increase efficiency, provided that efficiency is closely linked to contribution rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly, its value is primarily in establishing the behavioral mechanism and the robustness of the punishment effect on cooperation. It also highlights that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as group size and information structure, are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative estimates for these effects on efficiency.

The review also discusses the concept of strong reciprocity, where individuals are willing to punish norm violators at a cost to themselves, and suggests that such mechanisms are critical for sustaining cooperation in groups. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or directly analyze efficiency or group payoff; instead, it synthesizes existing theoretical and empirical work to propose directions for future research.

However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will increase cooperation and likely improve group outcomes, but it does not provide the detailed, dimension-specific evidence needed for precise prediction of efficiency changes.

  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, cooperation typically declines over time due to negative affect and conditional cooperation. However, when a punishment option is introduced, cooperation rates remain high, as punishment deters defection. The motivation to punish is linked to affective responses, such as anger at defectors and satisfaction from punishing. The paper notes that punishment can sometimes be misapplied (e.g., punishing cooperators), which can undermine cooperation. The findings are based on a synthesis of prior experimental studies, not new empirical data, and focus on behavioral and affective outcomes rather than direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support for the idea that introducing punishment in public goods games can sustain high cooperation rates and prevent the typical decline seen in repeated games without punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report new experimental results. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of a punishment mechanism is likely to increase cooperation and, by implication, efficiency, provided punishment is targeted at defectors. The review also cautions that punishment can be counterproductive if misapplied. The paper is most useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators of punishment effects, but less so for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper argues that fairness-based motivation leads to punishment of norm violations, which can sustain cooperation in public goods games, as shown in referenced empirical studies. Compassion-based motivation, in contrast, can prevent the breakdown of cooperation in noisy environments by promoting forgiveness, but may not be effective in the long term against persistent defectors. The emotional and neural mechanisms underlying these motivations are distinct. The paper does not provide new empirical results or direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, but summarizes that punishment options in public goods games are associated with higher cooperation rates and norm enforcement.

However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and thus likely efficiency), but does not allow for fine-grained prediction based on game design dimensions. It is most useful for understanding the motivational and emotional mechanisms behind punishment and cooperation, and for contextualizing why punishment may be effective in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Jin, S. X., Spadaro, G., & Balliet, D. (2025). Institutions and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis of Structural Features in Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment (and/or reward) in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games robustly increases cooperation rates. The meta-analytic effect size for punishment is d = 0.60 (Study 2), indicating a substantial increase in cooperation when punishment is available. The effect is stronger in repeated interactions (partner matching) than in one-shot (stranger matching) games. Communication is also highly effective. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or net welfare, and notes that prior research (e.g., Bochet et al., 2006) found that punishment may increase contributions but not efficiency due to its cost. Thus, while the evidence for increased cooperation is strong, the effect on efficiency is not directly addressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games increases cooperation rates, especially in repeated interactions. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment is likely to increase average contributions. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform whether punishment increases, decreases, or leaves unchanged the efficiency of the group (i.e., total payoff relative to full cooperation). The costliness of punishment is not analyzed in relation to efficiency. Therefore, the paper is highly informative for predicting behavioral cooperation, but only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuitive processing (via time pressure, ego depletion, conceptual primes, etc.) increases negative reciprocity (i.e., punishment of norm violations) in economic games, including public goods games with punishment phases and ultimatum games. This is supported by a meta-analysis showing a small but significant increase in punishment/rejection rates when intuition is promoted. However, the review does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is not addressed; the focus is on the cognitive basis of the decision to punish, not on the downstream consequences for group outcomes.

For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this review is not directly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or welfare outcomes. It is useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior (e.g., cognitive manipulation, individual differences), but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Use this paper to inform models of punishment frequency or behavioral response, not efficiency.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Human cooperation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and experimental literature on the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in humans, with a focus on public goods games and related social dilemmas. It finds that: (1) Repetition and targeted reciprocity (including punishment and reward) can promote cooperation in public goods games; (2) Both punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but reward is generally more efficient (i.e., produces higher group payoffs) than punishment, which can destroy value and provoke retaliation or antisocial punishment; (3) The effectiveness of punishment and reward depends on their implementation and the broader social context; (4) Cooperation in one-shot anonymous games is explained as an overgeneralization of strategies learned in repeated or reputation-based settings. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and qualitative evidence that adding punishment to public goods games can increase cooperation, but may not always increase efficiency or group payoff, especially if punishment is costly, provokes retaliation, or is used antisocially. Reward mechanisms are generally more efficient than punishment. The review does not provide quantitative data or direct predictions about efficiency changes, so it should be used for qualitative guidance: expect that punishment can promote cooperation, but its effect on efficiency is context-dependent and may be negative if punishment is misapplied. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the design of punishment (cost, effectiveness, possibility of antisocial use) and the presence of reward options are important moderators.

- source: Henrich, J., & Muthukrishna, M. (2021). The Origins and Psychology of Human Cooperation. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 72*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The review argues that mechanisms such as punishment, reputation, and signaling are crucial for sustaining cooperation in large-scale human societies, especially where kinship and direct reciprocity are insufficient. It highlights that the willingness to punish norm violations (including in public goods and related games) varies dramatically across societies and is associated with community size and institutional context. The review synthesizes evidence that punishment can sustain cooperation, but also that the form and prevalence of punishment are culturally variable. It also notes that mechanisms supporting cooperation can stabilize both prosocial and maladaptive costly norms. However, the review does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can sustain cooperation in public goods game-like environments, especially in large, anonymous societies. It also highlights that the effectiveness and prevalence of punishment depend on cultural, institutional, and demographic factors such as community size. However, because the review does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in identifying which dimensions (e.g., community size, presence of punishment, cultural context) are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, and in providing context for interpreting empirical findings from other studies.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation and punishment behaviors in economic games, including public goods games, are guided by evolved fairness norms shaped by partner choice. It reviews evidence that people punish both low and high contributors in PGGs, often to restore fairness rather than to maximize group efficiency. The mutualistic approach predicts that individuals seek proportionality between contributions and rewards, and that punishment is used to rebalance unfair distributions. The paper notes that in real-world small-scale societies, exclusion and partner switching are more common than costly punishment. It also highlights that punishment in PGGs is sometimes antisocial (targeting high contributors) and is strongly shaped by local fairness norms. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and interpretive context for understanding why and how punishment may affect cooperation and fairness in PGG-like environments, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as fair and proportional, and that exclusion or partner choice may be more effective than costly punishment in sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., fairness norms, entitlement, partner choice), but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

  findings: The paper argues that status hierarchies can endogenously coordinate punishment in public goods games, leading to more optimal (i.e., less costly and more effective) use of punishment. High status actors are more likely to be expected to punish when punishment is seen as group-oriented, and their punishment is more likely to promote subsequent cooperation and reduce the need for further punishment. The author suggests that these dynamics can help groups realize public goods more efficiently, but this is a theoretical claim rather than an empirically demonstrated effect. The paper also discusses why typical experimental designs may fail to observe these effects, due to their suppression of status emergence and communication.

However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that game designs allowing status differentiation, communication, or leadership roles may see more efficient punishment use, but this is not quantified. The paper is less useful for direct prediction of efficiency from game design dimensions in the absence of empirical data.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Kiyonari, T., Molenmaker, W. E., & van Dijk, E. (2019). Do people prefer leaders who enforce norms? Reputational effects of reward and punishment decisions in noisy social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across three experimental studies, the paper finds that both rewarding and punishing norm enforcement by leaders in public goods games (and an organizational scenario) generally improve third-party observers' perceptions of leader competence and morality, especially under conditions of perfect monitoring (no noise). Under behavioral noise (imperfect monitoring), the reputational benefits of punishment are reduced or eliminated, while rewarding continues to yield positive reputational effects. These effects extend to leader preference (i.e., observers are more likely to prefer leaders who reward or punish, especially reward, over those who do not enforce norms). However, the studies do not measure or report on actual group efficiency, payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to third-party observer perceptions of leader reputation and preference, not to actual behavioral or payoff outcomes among group members. While the design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost) are described, the outcomes are not efficiency-related. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may, however, inform hypotheses about the reputational incentives for leaders to use punishment or reward, which could indirectly affect norm enforcement behavior in other studies.
