  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with random rematching, the ability to gossip about others' contributions and to ostracize (exclude) group members based on this information leads to a substantial increase in cooperation and, after several rounds, to the highest group and individual earnings. Gossip alone also increases cooperation and earnings compared to a basic game with no reputation or exclusion, but the effect is less pronounced and decays over time. Ostracism is used primarily against low contributors, and those who are ostracized tend to increase their contributions upon returning. The efficiency gains from gossip and ostracism are not immediate, as exclusion temporarily reduces group size and the public good multiplier, but by the final rounds, these groups outperform the others in earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a punishment-like mechanism (ostracism) in addition to reputation/gossip in a public goods game increases efficiency (group and individual earnings) compared to both a basic game and a gossip-only game. The effect is robust despite a temporary efficiency loss due to exclusion. The design dimensions—such as group size (4), number of rounds (6 per game), no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR of 0.5 (or 0.375 when group size drops to 3), and anonymous exclusion—are all specified, making this study highly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment (ostracism) in similar lab PGGs. The results suggest that, even when punishment is implemented as exclusion rather than direct costly punishment, and even when exclusion temporarily reduces group size and multiplier, the long-run effect on efficiency is positive if reputation information is available.

- source: Masclet, D. (2003). Ostracism in work teams: a public good experiment. *International Journal of Manpower*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) others after observing their contributions in a public good game leads to much higher contributions and significantly higher group payoffs compared to a control without exclusion. Both costless and costly exclusion treatments yield similar increases in efficiency, with group payoffs rising by about 28-30% relative to the control. Actual exclusions are rare, but the threat is sufficient to sustain cooperation. The effect is robust to whether exclusion is directly costly or not. The study provides direct evidence that non-monetary peer punishment (ostracism) can substantially improve efficiency in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling exclusion (ostracism) in a standard public good game with four players and continuous contributions leads to a large and robust increase in efficiency (group payoff), regardless of whether exclusion is directly costly or not. For prediction tasks, the presence of an exclusion mechanism (even if rarely used) should be expected to substantially increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, under similar game parameters (n=4, 10 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, continuous contributions, no rewards). The effect is observed even when the control efficiency is low and is robust to the cost structure of exclusion. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment-like mechanisms (ostracism) in similar lab PGG designs.

- source: Eichenseer, M. (2023). Leading-by-example in public goods experiments: What do we know?. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes 62 experimental studies (mainly linear public goods games) and conducts meta-analyses and weighted least squares regressions. It finds that sequential play (leadership) increases group efficiency compared to simultaneous play. Centralized punishment and reward (where only the leader can punish or reward) further increase efficiency, with punishment having a stronger effect than reward. Peer punishment/reward (where all can punish/reward) have even larger effects. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. The marginal per capita return (MPCR) and group size also positively affect efficiency, but to a lesser extent. Leaders contribute more than followers, and followers contribute about 79% as much as leaders. Inequality within groups does not generally increase with leadership. Leaders' payoffs are similar to those of average players in simultaneous games. The paper provides quantitative estimates of effect sizes for each moderator.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, quantitative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment (and reward) on efficiency in public goods games, conditional on game design dimensions. The meta-regressions include variables for group size, number of rounds, MPCR, presence and type of punishment/reward, and leadership structure. The results show that adding centralized punishment (by a leader) increases efficiency by about 0.26 (on a 0-1 scale of contributions relative to optimum), and peer punishment increases it by about 0.47. The effect of reward is positive but smaller. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. These findings can be used to adjust predicted efficiency when punishment is enabled, given the control efficiency and game design. The paper is highly relevant for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Goto, J., & Matsui, T. (2025). Counterproductive punishment in closed communities: Experimental evidence from Japanese fishery. *Marine Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, before punishment is introduced, average contributions are similar in closed and open communities. After introducing the (indirect, weak) punishment mechanism, contributions decrease substantially in closed communities (by 17 percentage points) and increase slightly in open communities (by 4 percentage points). Regression analyses confirm that the negative effect in closed communities is robust to controls and alternative specifications. The mechanism appears to be that punishment in closed communities is perceived as excessive peer pressure, undermining trust and cooperative norms. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the direction of contribution changes suggests that punishment may reduce efficiency in closed communities and slightly increase it in open communities.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment depends on community structure: in environments with strong pre-existing cooperative norms and high internal communication (closed communities), even weak, indirect punishment can reduce contributions and likely efficiency. In more open communities, punishment has a small positive effect. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates. The punishment mechanism is indirect and low-cost, so results may not generalize to direct, costly punishment systems. The findings are most relevant for settings with similar social structure and weak, reputation-based punishment.

- source: Kerr, N. L., Rumble, A. C., Park, E. S., Ouwerkerk, J. W., Parks, C. D., Gallucci, M., & van Lange, P. A. M. (2009). How many bad apples does it take to spoil the whole barrel?: Social exclusion and toleration for bad apples. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the presence of even a single 'bad apple' (defector) can sharply reduce cooperation among other group members when there is no threat of social exclusion. However, when group members can be excluded for uncooperative behavior (i.e., there is a credible threat of social exclusion), participants are much less likely to follow the bad apple's example, and cooperation remains high unless a majority of the group are defectors. This deterrent effect of exclusion is strong in small groups (4-5 people) but disappears in larger groups (8 people), where exclusion threats do not increase cooperation. The dependent variable is contribution/cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, but the findings imply that exclusion threats would increase group efficiency in small groups.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social exclusion mechanism (a form of non-monetary punishment) in small public goods games (4-5 players) increases cooperation and would likely increase efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is degraded by the presence of defectors. However, the effect of exclusion threats disappears in larger groups (8 players), suggesting that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment via exclusion is moderated by group size. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from cooperation rates. The exclusion mechanism studied is not monetary punishment but social ostracism, so results may not generalize to all punishment technologies.

- source: Sell, J., & Wilson, R. K. (1999). The maintenance of cooperation: Expectations of future interaction and the trigger of group punishment. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that requiring a grim trigger punishment rule (where defection locks a player into permanent defection) substantially increases cooperation rates in a repeated public goods game. The effect of the discount parameter (expected number of rounds) on cooperation is only significant when the grim trigger is imposed, and only between the highest and lower values. Without a mandated punishment phase, increasing the discount parameter does not increase cooperation. The main dependent variable is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that group-level punishment threats can coordinate expectations and sustain cooperation, but the value of future interaction alone is insufficient unless paired with credible punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the imposition of a group-level, long punishment phase (grim trigger) increases cooperation rates in repeated public goods games, especially in the early rounds. However, the outcome measured is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling a strong group punishment mechanism (even if not individual-targeted) will increase cooperation and likely improve efficiency, especially when players expect many rounds. The effect of expected future interaction (discount parameter) is only realized when punishment is credible and severe. For prediction tasks, the presence of a mandated, long punishment phase should be treated as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, but the absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative inference.

- source: Reuben, E., & Tyran, J. R. (2010). Everyone is a winner: Promoting cooperation through all-can-win intergroup competition. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing tolerance-based expulsion in a spatial public goods game can substantially increase the level of cooperation (measured as the fraction of cooperators and expellers). The effect is strongest at intermediate tolerance thresholds and when expulsion costs are high. The mechanism allows expellers to balance the cost of expulsion with the benefit of removing defectors, leading to higher and more stable cooperation levels. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that tolerance-based expulsion mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially when the cost of expulsion is high and the tolerance threshold is optimally set. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation levels rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that enabling punishment (in the form of expulsion) is likely to increase cooperation, but the impact on efficiency is not directly quantified and may depend on the costliness of expulsion and the structure of the population. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Milinski, M. (2004). Strategic investment in reputation. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: This experiment demonstrates that the possibility of reputation transfer between a public goods game and an indirect reciprocity game leads to higher cooperation and significantly higher average payoffs in the public goods game. When players' identities are consistent across both games (transferable name), they invest more in cooperation, resulting in higher group earnings. When identities are not transferable, cooperation and payoffs decline. The manipulation of reputation transferability is the key treatment; no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The findings show that reputation incentives can substitute for punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling reputation transfer between a public goods game and another social context (indirect reciprocity game) increases efficiency, as measured by average payoff per player. While the study does not manipulate punishment, it is highly informative for prediction tasks involving reputation mechanisms as an alternative to punishment. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, number of rounds, information structure) are clearly specified, and the efficiency outcomes are directly reported. For prediction, this paper supports the view that mechanisms increasing the salience or transferability of reputation can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, even in the absence of punishment.

- source: Kitts, J. A. (2006). Collective action, rival incentives, and the emergence of antisocial norms. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective action and group efficiency in public-goods-like environments are shaped by a three-way interaction between the value of selective incentives (rewards), the rivalness of those incentives, and the strength of peer influence (cohesiveness). When incentives are nonrival, increasing their value or increasing peer influence both increase participation and group welfare. When incentives are rival, however, strong peer influence can lead to the enforcement of antisocial norms that discourage contribution, reducing group efficiency. The model demonstrates that under high rivalness and high incentive value, increasing peer influence or incentive value can actually decrease collective action and group welfare. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are most applicable to small groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, rival selective incentives and norm enforcement) on group efficiency is highly contingent on the design dimensions of the incentive system and the strength of peer influence. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling strong peer enforcement or increasing the value of selective incentives will not always increase efficiency—if the incentives are rival, these interventions can backfire and reduce group welfare. The model is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward-based incentives, especially when those rewards are rival. While the paper does not model punishment per se, the logic and results are highly informative for any system where sanctions or rewards are rival and peer enforcement is possible.

- source: Hamman, J. R., Weber, R. A., & Woon, J. (2011). An Experimental Investigation of Electoral Delegation and the Provision of Public Goods. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Electoral delegation, where a group elects a member to allocate contributions in a linear public goods game, results in sustained high efficiency and group payoffs, with most groups achieving or nearly achieving the social optimum. In contrast, standard voluntary contribution games (with or without communication) see contributions and efficiency decline to near zero. Communication boosts efficiency, but only when paired with delegation does it sustain high contributions. When delegation is endogenous (players can opt in), high efficiency is only achieved if communication is allowed, as it facilitates coordination and trust. The main mechanism is the selection of prosocial allocators and the ability to sanction selfish ones. Majority tyranny (where a coalition exploits a minority) is observed but infrequent. The results show that democratic delegation can overcome the free-rider problem and lead to efficient public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with a low MPCR and large groups, introducing a democratic delegation mechanism (electoral selection of a central allocator) dramatically increases efficiency compared to standard voluntary contribution mechanisms. Communication further enhances this effect, especially when delegation is endogenous. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of electoral delegation (even without explicit punishment) can substitute for or outperform punishment in achieving high efficiency, especially when communication is allowed. The control efficiency (voluntary contribution) is very low in this design, but efficiency with delegation is near the social optimum. The results are most applicable to settings with large groups, low MPCR, and centralized decision-making via election.

- source: Gërxhani, K., Volker, B., & Van Breemen, J. (2021). Who Will Do It? Volunteering to Change Cooperation Rules in a Heterogeneous Population. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Changing the minimum contribution rule from low to high in a public goods game increases both individual and collective earnings for all group members, regardless of their MPCR. The effect is robust and statistically significant. The likelihood of attempting a rule change depends on both social value orientation and benefit level: high-earning proselfs are most likely to initiate a change, while prosocials' behavior depends on whether they are high or low earners. The rule change also increases inequality between high and low earners. The experiment does not include peer punishment or reward mechanisms; the only 'cost' is the fee to initiate a rule change vote.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that increasing the minimum required contribution in a public goods game (via a formal rule change) increases group efficiency and total earnings, even in the presence of heterogeneity in MPCR. The findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of institutional changes that increase minimum cooperation requirements, but less directly relevant for peer punishment interventions. The absence of peer punishment or reward means the results are most applicable to settings where institutional rules, rather than decentralized sanctions, are the main intervention. The efficiency gain from raising the minimum contribution is clear and robust, suggesting that, in similar designs, enabling a mechanism to increase minimum contributions can substantially improve efficiency.

- source: Zschache, J. (2012). Producing public goods in networks: Some effects of social comparison and endogenous network change. *Social Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on networks, the ability of agents to change their social ties in response to social comparison (i.e., to seek out like-minded cooperators and avoid defectors) can sustain or increase cooperation and thus the production of public goods. Low network density and low costs of network change (low cohesion) allow cooperators to persist by forming supportive subgroups, leading to higher efficiency. High initial cooperation and low segregation also promote full cooperation. When network change is costly (high cohesion), cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model shows that both forward-looking and backward-looking (adaptive) agents can achieve high efficiency under favorable network conditions, but backward-looking agents require even more favorable starting conditions. Explicit costly punishment is not modeled; instead, social comparison provides a form of endogenous, non-costly social pressure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that endogenous network change and social comparison can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game designs allowing for dynamic partner selection or network rewiring (low cohesion/costs) will see higher efficiency, especially when initial cooperation is high and the network is not highly segregated. However, the absence of explicit costly punishment means the results are most applicable to settings where social comparison or endogenous social pressure is the main incentive, not where formal punishment is enabled. The results are directly informative for predicting efficiency in networked PGGs with endogenous tie change, but less so for classic PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Milinski, M. (2005). Reputation is valuable within and outside one's own social group. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study demonstrates that reputation built through cooperation in a public goods game increases the probability of being helped in subsequent indirect reciprocity rounds, and this effect is equally strong whether the helper is from the same or a different PGG group. Cooperation in the PGG is maintained at a high level while IR rounds are expected, but drops sharply when only PGG rounds remain. The experiment does not include punishment or sanctioning options, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, show that reputation-based reward mechanisms (via indirect reciprocity) can sustain high cooperation rates in PGGs, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment. The findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and reward can substitute for or complement punishment, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment and lack of efficiency outcomes limit the paper's direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., Holt, C. A., Rodgers, M. S., & Thomas-Hunt, M. C. (2020). The impact of leader communication on free-riding: An incentivized experiment with empowering and directive styles. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that empowering leader messages reduce free-riding (increase contributions) more than directive messages when there is no two-way leader-follower communication. When two-way communication is allowed, both leadership styles lead to higher contributions, and the difference between styles disappears. The presence of two-way communication is especially beneficial for directive leadership. The main dependent variable is contribution rate; earnings are reported but not the focus. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that communication (especially two-way leader-follower communication) can increase contributions, which may be relevant for understanding the context in which punishment is or is not necessary. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, communication, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means the findings are only indirectly relevant for the prediction task.

- source: Grayson, S., Feinberg, M., Willer, R., & Zaki, J. (2025). Ironic effects of prosocial gossip in driving inaccurate social perceptions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game, participants overwhelmingly use gossip to highlight free-riders rather than cooperators, even when most group members are cooperative. When a new set of participants reads these gossip notes, they systematically underestimate the cooperativeness of the original group, becoming more cynical and less accurate in their perceptions. However, this increased cynicism does not translate into lower expectations for their own group's cooperation or reduced contributions in their own public goods game. There is some evidence that exposure to negative gossip polarizes behavior, increasing the likelihood of both full cooperation and full defection, but the average contribution is not significantly affected.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or gossip on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it provides evidence that gossip (as a reputational mechanism) is negatively skewed and can distort outsiders' perceptions of group cooperativeness, but does not affect actual contribution behavior in subsequent games. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that reputational mechanisms like gossip may increase cooperation (as shown in the referenced Feinberg et al., 2014 study), but the current paper's main contribution is to highlight the informational and perceptual side effects of such mechanisms, not their impact on efficiency.

- source: Henrich, J., Boyd, R., Bowles, S., Camerer, C., Fehr, E., Gintis, H., McElreath, R., Alvard, M., Barr, A., Ensminger, J., Henrich, N. S., Hill, K., Gil-White, F., Gurven, M., Marlowe, F. W., Patton, J. Q., & Tracer, D. (2005). Economic man in cross-cultural perspective: Behavioral experiments in 15 small-scale societies. *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the canonical model of self-interest fails to predict behavior in all societies studied. There is substantial cross-cultural variation in offers and contributions in ultimatum, dictator, and public goods games. Group-level variables such as market integration and payoffs to cooperation are strong predictors of prosocial behavior in these games. However, the study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the public goods games, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main findings are about the determinants of prosocial behavior, not about the effects of punishment or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. It does provide informative context about baseline contribution rates and the influence of group-level variables on prosocial behavior in public goods games across diverse societies. However, for the downstream prediction task of estimating efficiency with and without punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or report on punishment or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Stoop, J., van Soest, D., & Vyrastekova, J. (2018). Rewards and cooperation in social dilemma games. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that net-positive rewards (where the recipient gains more than the sender loses) dramatically increase cooperation and efficiency in standard public goods games (VCM), with most groups reaching high contribution levels when rewards are available. In contrast, in the Claim game—where players can undo others' contributions by taking from the public pool—rewards have only a marginal effect on cooperation, and average net contributions are not significantly different from zero. In the Claim game, rewards are often exchanged bilaterally and are not strongly linked to cooperative behavior in the social dilemma. The results suggest that the effectiveness of rewards in promoting efficiency depends critically on the structure of the social dilemma, particularly whether others can undo cooperative actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms in increasing efficiency in public goods games depends on whether the game allows for negative actions (i.e., taking from the public pool) and whether the MPCR is certain. In standard VCMs (linear PGGs), rewards can substantially increase efficiency, but in environments where contributions can be undone (as in the Claim game or CPR games), rewards are much less effective. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'take' option or uncertain MPCR is a critical moderator: control efficiency is not a sufficient predictor of treatment efficiency with rewards if the game allows for undoing others' contributions. The findings do not inform about punishment effects, as punishment is not studied.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Coevolutionary dynamics of population and institutional rewards in public goods games. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional rewards, if the reward strength is adaptively increased when cooperation is low and decreased when cooperation is high, the system can reach stable equilibria with sustained cooperation and group welfare. The model shows that: (1) a stable coexistence of cooperation and reward strength is possible; (2) full cooperation can be achieved with minimal reward intensity if the per capita incentive is above a threshold; (3) moderate cooperation can be sustained with either low or high reward intensity, depending on parameter values; (4) in some cases, even high reward intensity cannot prevent full defection if incentives are too low. The model provides explicit conditions for these outcomes in terms of group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with institutional rewards, but not for games with punishment. It provides theoretical predictions for how adaptive reward mechanisms affect group payoff and cooperation, given game parameters such as group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward strength. However, it does not address punishment, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding the efficiency impact of reward-based interventions, especially when reward strength is allowed to adapt to cooperation levels.

- source: Alvarez-Rodriguez, U., Battiston, F., de Arruda, G. F., Moreno, Y., Perc, M., & Latora, V. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of higher-order interactions in social networks. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in PGGs with higher-order interactions (hypergraphs), the critical synergy factor required for cooperation decreases as group size increases in uniform random hypergraphs, meaning larger groups can foster cooperation more easily (at a given synergy factor). Heterogeneity in group membership (hyperdegree) and group size can either facilitate or hinder cooperation, depending on the structure: scale-free (hub-dominated) hypergraphs inhibit cooperation, while uniform or less heterogeneous structures do not. The framework is validated with empirical data from scientific collaborations, showing that the synergy factor (group benefit) is a non-monotonic function of group size, with an optimal group size for maximum benefit. The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency and payoff outcomes in PGG-like environments with varying group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. It is directly relevant for understanding how group size and network heterogeneity affect the critical conditions for cooperation and group payoff, but cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. Use this paper to inform baseline efficiency predictions in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but not for treatment effects of punishment.

- source: van Soest, D., Stoop, J., & Vyrastekova, J. (2016). Toward a delineation of the circumstances in which cooperation can be sustained in environmental and resource problems. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game (VCM), average net group contributions are positive and significant, leading to substantial group payoffs and efficiency. In contrast, when the action space is extended to allow negative contributions (claims/taking from the public fund), average net group contributions are not significantly different from zero, and group payoffs are much lower. This holds even for subjects who initially display prosocial behavior. The ability to undo others' contributions destroys cooperation and efficiency, and the effect is not explained by framing or subject pool differences. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the action space—specifically, whether players can undo others' contributions—has a dramatic effect on efficiency in public goods games. In standard VCMs (with only non-negative contributions), efficiency is much higher than in Claim Games (where negative contributions are allowed). However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, establish a strong baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is high in standard VCMs and very low in Claim Games. For prediction tasks, this paper helps set expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency under different action space designs, but does not inform the effect of punishment.

- source: Windmann, S., Binder, L., Bender, C., Langenbucher, K., Vollmeyer, R., & Schultze, M. (2024). Facets of altruism in study majors and volunteering groups. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that allowing heterogeneous investment and dynamic feedback on the multiplication factor in spatial public goods games can substantially increase both cooperation and group-level success (measured by average payoff and group success). High feedback strength and moderate group-preference/half-success effort are optimal for group success and individual welfare. The model recovers the standard spatial PGG when feedback is disabled. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are included or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for understanding how dynamic feedback and heterogeneous investment affect efficiency and payoff in public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. Its findings may be useful for predicting efficiency in PGGs with dynamic returns, but not for the specific prediction task of punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency. It provides no evidence on punishment, so should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., Ross-Gillespie, A., & West, S. A. (2010). Cooperation in humans: competition between groups and proximate emotions. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing group competition into a standard public goods game increases individual contributions, but does not increase individual earnings. In the standard PGG (no group competition), higher contributions are associated with lower individual income, while in the group competition treatment, there is no significant relationship between contribution and income. The paper focuses on behavioral outcomes (contribution, emotions, perceptions of group mates) rather than efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The findings may be useful for understanding the effect of intergroup competition on cooperation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Vermeer, A. B. L., Heerema, R. L., & Sanfey, A. G. (2016). Third-Party Cooperation: How Reducing Material Involvement Enhances Contributions to the Public Good. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that reducing personal material involvement in a public goods game increases cooperation rates: participants contribute more when making decisions for a third party than for themselves. This effect is further enhanced when the group consists of human players rather than computer players, suggesting that social motives drive third-party cooperation. The main outcome is the proportion of tokens contributed, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how personal involvement and social context affect cooperation rates, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. The design and results may be contextually informative for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Griessmair, M., & Hippmann, P. (2022). Anger, Guilt, and Repeated Cooperation in Social Dilemmas. *Emotion*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated public goods games, experienced anger (arising when others contribute less than expected) leads to a greater decline in contributions, while experienced guilt (arising when one contributes less than others or less than expected) cushions this decline. These effects are robust to controlling for cognitive-economic variables (beliefs about others' contributions). The effects of anger and guilt differ by cooperative type: free riders act on anger but not guilt, while cooperators and reciprocators act on both. The emotional effects are present in both partner and stranger matching, with some evidence for emotional carryover. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the baseline dynamics of cooperation in repeated linear PGGs without punishment or reward. It shows that emotional responses (anger, guilt) are important drivers of contribution decline, but does not address the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding control (no-punishment) efficiency and the psychological mechanisms underlying contribution decline. It does not provide evidence on how punishment would affect efficiency, nor does it manipulate any of the key game design dimensions related to punishment or reward.

- source: Smith, E. A. (2010). Communication and collective action: language and the evolution of human cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants' contributions in a one-shot, sequential PGG are strongly influenced by the observed contributions of their group members, consistent with strategic reciprocation. Individual differences in motivational traits matter: higher BAS Reward Responsiveness predicts lower contributions when others have contributed highly (i.e., more self-regarding, strategic behavior), while higher BIS-anxiety predicts a lower likelihood of making zero contributions (i.e., less free riding). The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It is useful for understanding heterogeneity in contribution behavior and the role of personality traits in cooperation, but does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design is a standard sequential PGG with no punishment or reward options, so it is only relevant as a control or baseline for contribution behavior in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Barclay, P. (2006). Reputational benefits for altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants contribute more to a public good when they expect their reputation to matter in a subsequent trust game, and that contributions are maintained best when there is competition to be the most altruistic. Trust is preferentially given to those who contributed more in the PGG. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The outcomes are primarily behavioral (contributions, trust), not efficiency or payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how reputation and competitive altruism can increase contributions, but does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. The design and findings are relevant for reputation and trust mechanisms, not for punishment or sanctioning treatments.

- source: Naar, N. (2020). Gaming Anthropology: The Problem of External Validity and the Challenge of Interpreting Experimental Games. *American Anthropologist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that experimental economic games, including public goods games, show substantial variability in behavioral outcomes (such as cooperation and prosociality) across populations, subject pools, settings, and contextual frames. There is no reliable generalizability of results, and evidence for parallelism between game and real-world behavior is mixed. The review highlights the importance of context and framing in interpreting game results and cautions against assuming that game behavior reflects real-world preferences or norms. The paper does not address punishment or efficiency outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It is useful for understanding the limitations of generalizing behavioral results from experimental games, especially across different populations and contexts. However, it does not discuss punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and thus offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is as a caution about external validity and the importance of context, not as a source of quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or payoff effects.

- source: OSTROM, E., WALKER, J., & GARDNER, R. (1992). COVENANTS WITH AND WITHOUT A SWORD - SELF-GOVERNANCE IS POSSIBLE. *American Political Science Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a CPR game with 8 players and a quadratic production function, baseline efficiency (net yield) is low, especially in high-endowment settings. Allowing communication (covenants) alone increases efficiency substantially, with repeated communication leading to near-optimal outcomes. Introducing a sanctioning mechanism (the sword) alone increases net yield before costs, but after accounting for the costs of punishment, efficiency gains are minimal or negative. When both communication and sanctioning are allowed, especially when the group can choose the sanctioning mechanism, efficiency is maximized and defection rates are minimized. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus highly context-dependent: punishment alone is not efficient, but when combined with communication and endogenous institution-building, it is highly effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the presence of communication and the way the punishment institution is implemented. If the game design allows for communication and endogenous choice of punishment, enabling punishment can dramatically increase efficiency. If punishment is imposed without communication, efficiency gains are small or negative after accounting for punishment costs. The control efficiency (without punishment) is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect of punishment is positive only when paired with communication and institutional choice. Game design dimensions such as player count (8), number of rounds (20+), absence of chat in baseline, continuous contribution, and the specific punishment cost/tech parameters are all relevant for prediction.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). When sanctions fail to increase cooperation in social dilemmas: Considering the presence of an alternative option to defect. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the authors find that introducing a sanctioning system (punishment for defection) increases collective outcomes (efficiency) in a standard two-option social dilemma (cooperate or defect). However, when a third option to defect (alternative defection, more harmful to the group) is available, sanctions do not increase efficiency and can even decrease it, particularly when participants have low expectations of others' cooperation. The negative effect is mediated by the failure of sanctions to increase trust or expectations of cooperation in the presence of the third option. Thus, the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is contingent on the decision structure and social expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is not uniform: it is positive in standard two-choice settings but can be null or negative when alternative, more harmful defection options are available and when group members have low expectations of cooperation. For prediction, this means that the presence of alternative defection options (i.e., more than two choices, with at least one being more harmful to the group) and low trust/expectations are critical moderators. If the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency and the game allows for alternative defection, enabling punishment may not improve and could even worsen efficiency, especially if group trust is low. In standard two-choice games, punishment is likely to increase efficiency.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2013). Empowering neighbors versus imposing regulations: An experimental analysis of pollution reduction schemes. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a non-point source pollution problem with heterogeneous agents, four treatments were compared: baseline (no intervention), group tax only, punishment only, and group tax plus punishment. The group tax mechanism (formal regulation) led to large and statistically significant increases in efficiency (from ~55% in baseline to ~87% with tax, and ~90% with tax plus punishment). Peer punishment alone increased efficiency only slightly and not significantly. When combined, punishment reduced variance and helped keep pollution below the target more often, but the efficiency gain was offset by the deadweight loss from punishment costs. Punishment was used less frequently and less intensely than in standard public goods games, limiting its effectiveness. The results suggest that formal regulatory mechanisms are more effective than informal peer punishment in increasing efficiency, but peer punishment can play a complementary role.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with a negative externality (pollution), enabling peer punishment alone yields only modest and inconsistent efficiency gains, while a formal group penalty (tax) mechanism yields large and robust efficiency improvements. Adding punishment to the group tax further reduces damages and increases the likelihood of meeting the target, but does not significantly increase efficiency due to the costliness of punishment. For prediction, the presence of a formal group penalty is the key driver of efficiency, and the marginal effect of enabling peer punishment is small or negligible unless punishment is much more frequent or severe than observed here. The game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, rounds, information) are well-documented, making this study highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar settings.

- source: Milinski, M., & Marotzke, J. (2022). Economic experiments support Ostrom's polycentric approach to mitigating climate change. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab experiment with groups of 9 players, subdivided into 3 subgroups of 3, the opportunity for subgroups to impose costly sanctions on other subgroups (punishment treatment) did not increase the probability of reaching the collective target or improve group payoffs compared to a control without sanctions. Sanctioning was used frequently, but its costs reduced overall payoffs, and it failed to discipline free riders effectively because fair contributors who were also sanctioned reduced their own contributions. Thus, punishment in this polycentric, subgroup-based setting did not improve efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-goods-like collective-risk game with subgroup-level costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase group efficiency or the probability of reaching the collective target, and may even reduce efficiency due to the costs of punishment and negative effects on fair contributors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar designs (polycentric, subgroup-based, punishment between subgroups, costly punishment), enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency over the control condition without punishment, and may even be detrimental.

- source: Tarui, N., Mason, C. F., Polasky, S., & Ellis, G. (2008). Cooperation in the commons with unobservable actions. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common property resource game with unobservable actions, a two-part symmetric group punishment strategy (harsh group punishment after a defection, then forgiveness and return to cooperation) can support full cooperation (first-best efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, provided certain conditions on the discount factor, resource growth rate, and group size are met. The analysis shows that the threat of the harshest possible group punishment (worst perfect equilibrium) is sufficient to deter defection, even when individual actions are not observable. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when this is possible and uses numerical simulations to illustrate how the sustainability of cooperation depends on group size and other parameters. Notably, the effect of group size is non-monotonic: increasing group size can initially make cooperation easier to sustain (by making punishment more credible), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect eventually dominates, making cooperation harder to sustain.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in public-goods-like environments with unobservable actions, the availability of credible, symmetric group punishment can enable full efficiency (relative to the first-best cooperative outcome), provided the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the parameter conditions (discount factor, resource growth, group size) are favorable. The analysis is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games where only aggregate outcomes are observable. The findings suggest that punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, but only if the punishment is credible and harsh enough to deter defection. The effect of group size is nuanced: increasing group size can initially make punishment more credible (and thus support efficiency), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect increases and efficiency becomes harder to sustain. The paper does not address other design dimensions such as chat, reward, or information feedback, so its predictive value is strongest for settings matching the model's assumptions (no chat, no reward, symmetric group punishment, unobservable individual actions, continuous contribution/harvest choices).

- source: ABREU, D., MILGROM, P., & PEARCE, D. (1991). INFORMATION AND TIMING IN REPEATED PARTNERSHIPS. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated partnership games with imperfect monitoring, the ability to sustain efficient cooperation through punishment is fundamentally limited by the informativeness of the monitoring technology and the timing of information. When monitoring is imperfect, reducing the interest rate (making players more patient) increases the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes, potentially allowing near-first-best payoffs. However, shortening the period of fixed action or reducing the lag with which information is reported can actually decrease efficiency, sometimes eliminating the possibility of cooperation altogether. Delaying information release can, counterintuitively, increase efficiency by reducing the cost of deterring deviations. The paper provides generalizations to n-player symmetric games and shows that these results are robust to more complex action and signal spaces.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public-goods-like games with punishment under imperfect monitoring. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the informativeness of monitoring, the timing of information, and the patience of players. In particular, enabling punishment does not guarantee high efficiency unless monitoring is sufficiently informative and the timing of information and action periods is chosen carefully. For prediction, the paper suggests that increasing patience (lower interest rate) is reliably associated with higher efficiency, but increasing the frequency of action or information updates can reduce efficiency when monitoring is imperfect. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to estimate efficiency given the relevant game design dimensions.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Shrestha, M. D. (2021). Evolving institutions for collective action by selective imitation and self-interested design. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of collective action with institutionalized punishment by leaders, both selective imitation and self-interested design (foresight) in leaders can lead to the emergence of effective institutions that increase cooperation, group production, and payoffs (efficiency). The presence of leaders who monitor and punish free-riders increases group effort and payoffs compared to groups without leaders or punishment. The effect is strongest when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high, punishment is not too costly, and leaders value future payoffs. The model also shows that the details of the updating mechanism (imitation vs. foresight) matter most when the benefit-to-cost ratio is intermediate and leaders heavily discount the future. In 'us vs. nature' games, increased monitoring and production lead to higher payoffs for all; in 'us vs. them' games, overproduction can reduce payoffs for subordinates. The model provides direct evidence that institutionalized punishment can increase efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling institutionalized punishment by leaders in public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and punishment is not too costly. The effect is robust to variations in group size, punishment cost, and the leader's share of rewards. For prediction tasks, the presence of leader-administered punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), with the magnitude depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio, punishment cost, and whether leaders value future payoffs. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is less positive or even negative if punishment is very costly or if leaders do not value future payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with institutionalized punishment, especially when the control efficiency is low due to free-riding.

- source: Farjam, M., Faillo, M., Sprinkhuizen-Kuyper, I., & Haselager, P. (2015). Punishment Mechanisms and Their Effect on Cooperation: A Simulation Study. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment can facilitate higher group efficiency (average energy) in environments where resources are scarce (low MPCR), but only when the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid antisocial punishment and minimize costs (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun). Unrestricted punishment consistently reduces efficiency. When resources are abundant (high MPCR), punishment mechanisms do not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to no-punishment. The results are robust to variations in group size and punishment cost ratio. The study provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by resource availability (analogous to MPCR in PGGs) and by the design of the punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in PGG-like environments depends critically on both the marginal per-capita return (MPCR, here operationalized as resource availability) and the specific punishment mechanism. When MPCR is low (resources scarce), enabling well-designed punishment (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun) increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but unrestricted punishment reduces efficiency. When MPCR is high (resources abundant), punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of punishment on efficiency should be modeled as conditional on both MPCR and the punishment mechanism. The results are robust to moderate changes in group size and punishment cost ratio, so these dimensions are less likely to moderate the direction of the effect.

- source: Nair, A., Blome, C., Choi, T. Y., & Lee, G. (2018). Re-visiting collaborative behavior in supply networks structural embeddedness and the influence of contextual changes and sanctions. *Journal of Purchasing and Supply Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated triadic supply network game with real managers, the introduction of sanctions (punishment) reduces both group allocation and average payoffs when the incentive structure favors cooperation (i.e., low temptation to defect). When the incentive structure is changed to increase the temptation to defect, sanctions increase group allocation compared to the no-sanction condition, but average payoffs remain lower than in the cooperative baseline. Sanctions are only effective at increasing group allocation when the underlying incentive structure is unfavorable to cooperation. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average payoffs are provided for each condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus negative in cooperative contexts and ambiguous (possibly less negative) in defect-prone contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like network games depends critically on the underlying incentive structure. When the baseline (control) efficiency is high (cooperative context), enabling punishment reduces efficiency. When the baseline efficiency is low (defection is tempting), enabling punishment can increase group allocation but does not fully restore efficiency/payoff to cooperative levels. For prediction, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is negative when control efficiency is high, and less negative or possibly weakly positive when control efficiency is low, but the overall efficiency with punishment rarely exceeds the cooperative baseline. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the payoff structure (temptation to defect), the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the information structure.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2016). The impact of personal responsibility on the (un)willingness to punish non-cooperation and reward cooperation. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that people are more reluctant to punish non-cooperators when they feel personally responsible (i.e., when deciding individually), but this reluctance is attenuated when responsibility is shared in a group. This effect is specific to punishment and does not apply to rewarding cooperation. The effect is mediated by feelings of personal responsibility and entitlement, not by external accountability. The experiments use a common resource dilemma with sanctioning opportunities, but do not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs. Thus, the findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral moderators of punishment willingness, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish non-cooperation is higher when punishment decisions are made jointly by groups rather than by individuals, due to reduced personal responsibility and increased entitlement. However, since the study does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or common resource games. The results are useful for predicting when punishment will be more or less likely to be used, but not for predicting the efficiency consequences of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment decision (individual vs. group) may moderate the behavioral use of punishment, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2014). On the willingness to costly reward cooperation and punish non-cooperation: The moderating role of type of social dilemma. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that participants are more likely to reward cooperative behavior than to punish non-cooperative behavior, and that this tendency is stronger in public good dilemmas (where cooperation involves giving up private property) than in common resource dilemmas (where cooperation involves refraining from taking collective property). When given a choice, most participants prefer to reward rather than punish. The size of rewards is also larger than the size of punishments, especially in public good dilemmas. The study does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that the preference for reward over punishment may have implications for collective welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good and common resource games. It shows that, when given a choice, people are more willing to reward than to punish, and that punishment is used less frequently and less intensely, especially in public good dilemmas. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the mere availability of punishment may not lead to high usage, especially if reward is also available, and that the type of social dilemma (public good vs. common resource) moderates willingness to punish. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in public good dilemmas if participants are reluctant to use it, particularly when reward is also an option.

- source: MCCUSKER, C., & CARNEVALE, P. J. (1995). FRAMING IN RESOURCE DILEMMAS - LOSS AVERSION AND THE MODERATING EFFECTS OF SANCTIONS. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.

  findings: The study finds that both penalty and reward sanction systems increase cooperation in resource dilemmas, with reward sanctions producing the highest cooperation. The difference in cooperation between 'give' and 'take' frames (i.e., public goods vs. commons) disappears when sanctions are present. The effect of sanctions is robust across two experiments, and the framing of the sanction (give vs. take) affects contributions to the sanction fund but not cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect of sanctions on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
