For the downstream prediction task, this paper indicates that the efficiency of the control game (without punishment) can be high if the production function is nonlinear and the group decision rule is majoritarian, even in the absence of punishment. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Therefore, it should be used as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in nonlinear group production environments.

- source: Janssen, M. A., DeCaro, D., & Lee, A. (2022). An Agent-Based Model of the Interaction between Inequality, Trust, and Communication in Common Pool Experiments. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial common-pool resource game with four players and no punishment or sanctioning, communication (chat) between rounds leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. Trust is modeled as a key mediator: communication increases trust, which reduces overharvesting and increases group earnings. The agent-based model, calibrated to experimental data, shows that a population with a majority of conditional cooperators (who respond to trust and inequality) best explains observed group outcomes. The model and data both show that communication leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use, even after communication ceases, due to persistent increases in trust. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, enabling communication (chat) in a common-pool resource game with four players and nine rounds leads to a large increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of chat/communication on efficiency, but not for predicting the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. The model and empirical results suggest that communication alone can drive high efficiency via trust, but do not inform the marginal effect of adding punishment to such a game. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is highly informative for the effect of communication and trust in similar environments.

- source: Hergueux, J., Henry, E., Benkler, Y., & Algan, Y. (2023). Social Exchange and the Reciprocity Roller Coaster: Evidence from the Life and Death of Virtual Teams. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study links laboratory measures of reciprocity (from a one-shot public goods game) to field measures of reciprocity and project-level outcomes in open-source software teams. It finds that, while teams with more highly reciprocal members can achieve higher success scores (activity percentile) if they survive, they are also more likely to fail. The mechanism is that reciprocity amplifies both positive and negative contribution dynamics: highly reciprocal teams excel when cooperation is high, but are more fragile and prone to collapse after negative shocks or periods of inactivity. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The outcomes are group performance and survival, not direct PGG efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding the dynamics of reciprocity and cooperation in repeated public goods-like environments without punishment. The absence of punishment is a key design feature. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, high reciprocity can lead to both high performance and high fragility, but do not inform how enabling punishment would affect efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful as a control case for environments without punishment.

- source: Chambers, C. R., & Baker, W. E. (2020). Robust Systems of Cooperation in the Presence of Rankings: How Displaying Prosocial Contributions Can Offset the Disruptive Effects of Performance Rankings. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing performance rankings tied to differential rewards causes a sharp and persistent decline in cooperation (measured as transfer rates) in a generalized reciprocity game. This effect is especially pronounced in groups that were highly cooperative before the intervention. When reputation information (about others' past giving) is also provided, the initial drop in cooperation is similar, but cooperation rates recover over time to pre-intervention levels. The mechanism appears to be that actors use reputation information to reward those who have been cooperative, especially when they perceive inequity. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding how performance rankings and reputation information affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but the outcome of interest is cooperation/transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for understanding the impact of competitive incentives and reputation mechanisms on cooperation.

- source: Tazelaar, M. J. A., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Ouwerkerk, J. W. (2004). How to cope with Noise, in social dilemmas: The benefits of communication. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that negative noise (randomly reducing the partner's intended contribution) significantly reduces cooperation and benign impressions in a repeated two-player give-some game. Allowing the partner to communicate about noise (explaining/apologizing for the noise) restores cooperation and benign impressions to levels indistinguishable from the no-noise baseline. These effects are especially pronounced for participants with low dispositional trust. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and psychological impressions, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide strong evidence that communication can mitigate the negative effects of noise on cooperation in repeated continuous-contribution dilemmas. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding the role of communication and noise, but not for the core prediction task regarding punishment.

- source: Barclay, P., & Barker, J. L. (2020). Greener Than Thou: People who protect the environment are more cooperative, compete to be environmental, and benefit from reputation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) people who engage in more pro-environmental behaviors are more likely to cooperate in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma; (2) people donate more to an environmental charity when their donations are observed, and even more when competing to be chosen as a partner; (3) observers preferentially choose and cooperate more with those who donated more to the environment; (4) the correlation between environmentalism and cooperation is partially mediated by concern for others. The study does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of observability and partner choice (reputation/competition) on cooperative and pro-environmental behavior in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For prediction tasks involving the effects of observability or partner choice on cooperation, the findings suggest that such mechanisms can increase cooperative behaviors, but no efficiency or payoff data are available.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., & Jeong, J. (2015). Decoding covert motivations of free riding and cooperation from multi-feature pattern analysis of EEG signals. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that neural oscillations measured by EEG immediately after result presentation can predict whether a participant will cooperate or free ride in the next round, across three different payoff structures that manipulate motivations (fear and greed). Behaviorally, free riding rates are highest in the standard condition, lower in the 'no fear' condition, and lowest in the 'no greed' condition. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how payoff structure (fear/greed) affects cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Kirkland, K., Van Lange, P. A. M., Charalambous, N., Strauss, C., & Bastian, B. (2024). Beauty Builds Cooperation by Increasing Moral Concern for the Environment. *Environment and Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that when participants believe their extraction from a common pool will have negative consequences for the environment (e.g., less money to a tree preservation charity), they take fewer points, indicating more cooperative/conservation behavior. This effect is stronger when the environmental consequence is paired with a beautiful image of a tree. The effect is mediated by increased concern for tree preservation and reduced concern for personal financial gain. Manipulating the anthropomorphic qualities of trees (e.g., emphasizing mind-like traits) increases perceived mind but does not further increase cooperation. The studies do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It does, however, provide informative evidence that non-monetary moral value and aesthetic framing can increase cooperative behavior in resource dilemma games similar to PGGs. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, the paper is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how non-monetary framing or environmental consequences can shift extraction behavior, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2017). Cooperative strategies outside the laboratory - evidence from a long-term large-N-study in five countries. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a context-rich, long-term, repeated threshold public goods environment (an online game), the distribution of cooperative strategies differs from laboratory public goods games: free riding is as common as in the lab, but high cooperation is less frequent and conditional cooperation is somewhat less common. These strategies are stable over time and across different group contexts. The results suggest that context matters for the prevalence of cooperative strategies, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor on the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the baseline prevalence and stability of cooperative strategies in a long-term, context-rich threshold public goods environment without punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in the absence of punishment, free riding remains common, high cooperation is rare, and conditional cooperation is less prevalent than in some lab settings. The findings may help calibrate expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar environments, but do not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Sotnik, G., Choporov, S., & Shannon, T. (2023). The Role of Social Identity in a Population's Adoption of Prosocial Common-Pool Behavior. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that social identity, especially when combined with high group commitment, expands the parameter space (pressure-synergy combinations) where prosocial common-pool behavior is fully adopted. At low population density, both high- and low-commitment social identity models increase adoption and speed to full adoption compared to the baseline. At high density, only high-commitment agents show a strong positive effect; low-commitment agents may even reduce adoption. The model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral adoption rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how social identity and group commitment might affect the adoption of prosocial behavior in PGG-like environments, but not for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The model does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Balliet, D., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2013). Trust, Conflict, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both state trust (expectations of others' cooperation) and dispositional trust are positively correlated with cooperation in social dilemmas. The strength of this relationship is moderated by the degree of conflict in the game: trust is a stronger predictor of cooperation when the conflict of interest is higher (lower index of cooperation). The type of social dilemma (public goods, prisoner's dilemma, resource dilemma) does not significantly moderate the trust-cooperation relationship. Other moderators (iterations, group size, country, intergroup vs. individual) are also explored. The paper does not analyze or report on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about how the structure of the game (degree of conflict, as measured by the index of cooperation) moderates the relationship between trust and cooperation. Since efficiency is not measured, and punishment is not studied, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, but may be useful for understanding how baseline cooperation varies with game structure in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ahmad, M. G., & Loch, C. (2020). What do the followers want? The core functions of leadership. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated two-player strategic games, allowing players to choose a punishment option (where one can assign a low-value payoff to the other after decisions are made) does not increase group efficiency. Instead, punishment is associated with lower group payoffs due to retaliation cycles and reduced cooperation. In all games, the average group benefit under punishment is lower than under individual decision-making or leadership. Leadership (especially recommending leadership) increases efficiency, while punishment governance leads to the lowest efficiency. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across games with and without monetary incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated games with public-goods-like social dilemmas, enabling punishment as a governance option (without central leadership) reduces group efficiency compared to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across different game structures and incentive types. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment option (with moderate cost and impact, as in this design) should be expected to decrease efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is decentralized and not embedded in a leadership structure.

- source: Langlois, C. C., & Langlois, J. P. P. (2004). Provisions for noncompliance and treaty value: A game theoretic perspective. *International Studies Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with possible unintentional noncompliance (noise), the design of punishment schemes has a large effect on long-run efficiency (treaty value). Probabilistic trigger schemes, which are analogous to mutual punishment phases after a deviation, are self-punishing and reduce efficiency because both the perpetrator and the victim suffer during punishment. Contrite tit-for-tat (CTFT) schemes, which allow for redress (compensation to the victim) and delay retaliation (generosity), achieve much higher efficiency by minimizing the time spent in mutual defection and ensuring that only the guilty party is punished. The paper provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for how efficiency declines with noise under each scheme, and shows that CTFT dominates trigger schemes in most of the relevant parameter space.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of the punishment scheme (especially whether punishment is self-punishing or allows for redress) is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated social dilemma games. If the punishment mechanism is costly to the punisher and involves mutual punishment phases (as in standard trigger schemes), efficiency is substantially reduced, especially as noise increases. If the punishment mechanism allows for redress (compensation to the victim) and avoids mutual punishment (as in CTFT), efficiency is much higher. The paper also shows that the speed of retaliation (trigger probability) and the expected duration of punishment phases are important: swifter, shorter punishments are more efficient, but only if they still deter defection. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment based on the punishment technology, cost structure, and whether redress is possible.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2011). What makes people cooperate? Individual differences in BAS/BIS predict strategic reciprocation in a public goods game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with super cooperators (who both punish defectors and reward cooperators), the ability of speculative defectors to identify and cooperate with super cooperators (i.e., higher information level) generally increases the prevalence of super cooperators and raises average social payoff (efficiency), provided the cost of punishment/reward is not too high. When the cost is high, increasing information can instead destroy super cooperators. The alliance between speculative defectors and super cooperators can insulate super cooperators from exploitation by unconditional cooperators, further supporting cooperation and efficiency. The model demonstrates that the introduction of punishment/reward mechanisms and information about others' strategies can substantially improve group efficiency compared to the baseline with only unconditional cooperators and defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatially structured prisoner's dilemma environments, enabling punishment (and reward) mechanisms—especially when paired with higher information about others' strategies—can increase group efficiency, as measured by average social payoff. The effect is strongest when the cost of punishment/reward is moderate or low. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games with similar structure, though the model is not a standard public goods game but a spatial PD with three strategies. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient information) should increase efficiency relative to the control. However, if the cost of punishment/reward is high, the effect can reverse. The model's focus on spatial structure, information, and cost of punishment/reward provides indirect but informative guidance for analogous PGG-like environments.

- source: Bendor, J., & Swistak, P. (2001). The evolution of norms. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated games of enforceable cooperation, only social norms (strategies that prescribe third-party punishment) can be evolutionarily stable. The presence of effective punishment is necessary for stability. Both efficient and inefficient social norms can be stable, but efficient (cooperative) norms are more robust to invasion and require a lower frequency to stabilize. The model predicts that, over time, groups are more likely to drift toward efficient, cooperative equilibria regulated by social norms, but inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria are also possible and robust. The findings are general for two-person repeated games and extend to sparse networks, provided third-party information is sufficiently available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the necessity of punishment (sanctions) for stable cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. It shows that the presence of punishment mechanisms is a necessary condition for the evolutionary stability of cooperative, efficient outcomes, but does not guarantee efficiency: inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria can also be stable. However, more efficient equilibria are more robust to invasion and require a lower threshold to stabilize. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in a repeated game with enforceable cooperation will generally increase the likelihood of stable cooperation and higher efficiency, but the actual efficiency achieved may depend on the specific norm that evolves. The robustness of efficient equilibria to invasion is higher, so in the long run, efficiency is more likely with punishment enabled, but inefficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to two-person repeated games, but the logic extends to larger groups and sparse networks if third-party information is available.

- source: Macleod, W. B., Lara, V. V., & Zehnder, C. (2025). Worker Empowerment and Subjective Evaluation: On Building an Effective Conflict Culture. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of enabling punishment (conflict) in a principal-agent game with subjective evaluation is highly context-dependent. In the baseline, communication, and agreement (code of conduct) experiments, allowing workers to punish managers for not paying bonuses does not increase efficiency: the costs of conflict outweigh any small increases in effort, and total surplus is lower or unchanged compared to the no-punishment condition. Workers and managers fail to coordinate on an efficiency-enhancing norm, and punishment is often misapplied. However, when conflicts are institutionalized through a formal grievance process (grievance experiment), workers and managers coordinate more on a pay-for-output norm, effort increases significantly, and total surplus is higher in the punishment-enabled condition. Thus, punishment can enhance efficiency, but only when supported by strong institutional framing and norm coordination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is not uniform and is highly sensitive to the institutional environment, especially the presence of mechanisms that coordinate norms (such as a grievance procedure). In environments lacking such coordination, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency due to the direct costs of conflict. In contrast, when a formal grievance process is present, punishment can increase both effort and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of institutionalized conflict resolution and norm-coordination mechanisms should be considered a key moderator. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment) unless the institutional context is specified.

- source: Thomadsen, R., & Bhardwaj, P. (2011). Cooperation in Games with Forgetfulness. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of forgetfulness on efficiency in repeated games with punishment is fundamentally ambiguous: it can either reduce or increase the set of discount factors and game parameters under which full cooperation (and thus maximum efficiency) is sustainable. When the stage game is such that the cooperative action always yields nonnegative payoffs, forgetfulness weakens punishment and makes cooperation harder to sustain, reducing efficiency. However, when the stage game allows for the possibility that a forgetful player can be punished below the minimax, forgetfulness can make severe punishments credible and thus expand the range of parameters supporting cooperation, increasing efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas and graphical illustrations for these effects in the repeated prisoner's dilemma and a franchising effort game. The results are theoretical and depend on the structure of the stage game and the probability of forgetfulness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games is not monotonic and can be moderated by cognitive or institutional features like forgetfulness. In environments where punishment is only as severe as the stage minimax, introducing forgetfulness will generally reduce the efficiency gains from punishment. However, in environments where forgetfulness allows for below-minimax punishments, punishment can become more effective at sustaining cooperation, potentially increasing efficiency beyond what is possible with perfect recall. For prediction tasks, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined solely from standard game design dimensions; one must also consider whether the environment allows for forgetfulness and whether the stage game payoffs permit below-minimax punishments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates but gives clear theoretical boundaries and mechanisms.

- source: Evans, R., & Thomas, J. P. (2001). Cooperation and punishment. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated common interest games (with no discounting), the mere presence of cooperative (nondraconian) strategies in the set of possible strategies does not guarantee efficient (Pareto-optimal) outcomes in equilibrium. However, if the set of possible strategies includes at least one 'draconian' strategy (i.e., a strategy that is willing to punish noncooperation to the maximum extent, such as min-maxing the opponent forever after a deviation), then all Nash equilibria are efficient. The paper also shows that this result is robust to certain computability restrictions and discusses the limits of these results under discounting and other technical assumptions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated games with a structure similar to public goods or common interest games, the availability of sufficiently severe punishment (i.e., the ability to impose maximal punishment at low or no cost) is both necessary and sufficient for punishment to induce efficient outcomes in equilibrium. If the game design allows for draconian punishment strategies, then enabling punishment should lead to high efficiency, regardless of the control efficiency. If only mild or 'cooperative' punishment is possible, efficiency gains are not guaranteed. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, infinite-horizon, undiscounted games, but the logic is relevant for understanding the role of punishment severity and credibility in other settings.

- source: Han, T. A., Lenaerts, T. C., Santos, F. C., & Pereira, L. M. (2022). Voluntary safety commitments provide an escape from over-regulation in AI development. *Technology in Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated two-player (and generalizable to N-player) innovation race game, unconditional punishment of risk-taking (unsafe) behavior can reduce social welfare by over-regulating and suppressing beneficial innovation when risk is low. Introducing voluntary, binding commitments to safe behavior—where only those who commit and then defect are punished—enables the system to achieve high efficiency: safety compliance prevails when it is collectively optimal, and innovation is not stifled when risk-taking is preferred. Both peer and institutional punishment mechanisms are effective if they are targeted only at commitment violators. The model shows that the efficiency of punishment (cost to punisher vs. impact on punished) modulates these effects, but the voluntary commitment mechanism robustly improves outcomes across parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms—specifically, whether punishment is unconditional or conditional on voluntary commitment—has a major impact on group efficiency in public-goods-like innovation dilemmas. For prediction tasks, the presence of voluntary, binding commitments with targeted punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to unconditional punishment, especially in environments where the collective optimum varies with risk. The model also highlights the importance of punishment cost and effectiveness, and shows that allowing players to opt out of commitment (and thus avoid punishment) can prevent over-regulation and welfare loss. These insights are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in games with similar structure, even if the setting is not a classic PGG.

- source: Libois, F. (2022). Success and failure of communities managing natural resources: Static and dynamic inefficiencies. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that introducing sanctions (punishment) can increase efficiency in CPR games by enabling the enforcement of rules that prevent over-extraction (static inefficiency) and/or promote conservation (dynamic inefficiency). The effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the strength of the sanctioning mechanism relative to the incentives to deviate from cooperation. When sanctions are strong enough, the first-best (fully efficient) outcome can be achieved. With limited sanctions, only partial improvements are possible, and sometimes increasing the resource's value or growth rate can make enforcement harder, reducing efficiency. The model also shows that the number of players, extraction cost, and inequality among players all moderate the effect of sanctions on efficiency. In particular, more players or greater inequality can make conservation harder to enforce, while higher extraction costs can make it easier. The model provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in CPR-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment depends on the interplay between the sanctioning technology (maximum enforceable sanction), the number of players, the resource's growth rate, extraction cost, and initial value. If sanctions are strong enough relative to the incentives to deviate, enabling rules can be enforced and efficiency increases. If not, efficiency may not improve, or may even decrease if resource value/growth increases incentives to defect. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the logic of how punishment affects efficiency is highly transferable. The paper is especially informative for settings where the punishment mechanism is exogenous and bounded, and where resource dynamics (growth, conservation) are important. For prediction, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on whether the sanctioning parameters cross key thresholds relative to the game's other parameters.

- source: Cai, G. S., & Kock, N. (2009). An evolutionary game theoretic perspective on e-collaboration: The collaboration effort and media relativeness. *European Journal of Operational Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player e-collaboration games, introducing or increasing social punishment for defection increases the likelihood of full cooperation, which is Pareto efficient and maximizes group payoff. When punishment is low, defection or asymmetric cooperation is stable; when punishment exceeds a threshold (typically half the cost of cooperation), full cooperation becomes the unique stable Nash equilibrium. This result holds in both discrete and continuous effort models. The paper also shows that the required punishment to induce cooperation decreases as collaboration efficiency increases. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium characterizations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling or increasing punishment in a two-player, public-goods-like game will increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong relative to the cost of cooperation. The analytic results specify the threshold at which punishment changes the equilibrium from defection to cooperation. While the model is for two players and not a standard PGG, the structure is closely related, and the findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in small-group cooperation games. The results are most applicable when the punishment mechanism is salient, effective, and the cost/benefit structure is similar to the model.

- source: Friedman, D., & Singh, N. (2004). Negative reciprocity - The coevolution of memes and genes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that negative reciprocity (punishment) can transform a standard social dilemma (Prisoner's Dilemma) from an inefficient all-defect equilibrium to a coordination game with a Pareto efficient all-cooperate equilibrium, provided the punishment motive is strong enough. However, individual willingness to punish is undermined by evolutionary free-rider problems, as the costs are borne individually but the benefits are shared. The key finding is that group-level enforcement of punishment norms (memes), using low-cost sanctions such as shunning or status reduction, can sustain the optimal level of negative reciprocity and thus maximize group fitness. The model predicts that the efficiency of cooperation increases with the strength and enforcement of punishment norms, and that these norms will coevolve with individual capacities and environmental hostility. The comparative statics suggest that efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly, more effective, and more strongly enforced by group norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when group-enforced) increases efficiency in social dilemmas, compared to control conditions without punishment. The model is based on a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, but the authors argue that the logic extends to n-person games like public goods games. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the rigor of group enforcement are key moderators of the efficiency gain from punishment. The model also predicts that efficiency gains from punishment are larger when punishment is less costly and more effective, and when group norms for enforcement are strong. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct evidence from public goods games.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2010). Honor among thieves: Cooperation as a strategic response to functional unpleasantness. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and high group efficiency can emerge and persist in populations where all individuals are fundamentally self-interested and prefer to cheat, as long as some are willing to punish cheaters and some are strategic enough to cooperate when the risk of being punished is high. The presence of 'Strategic Unpleasant' types—who punish cheaters and cooperate only to avoid punishment—can lead to equilibria where most or all players cooperate, resulting in high average payoffs (efficiency). The model provides explicit payoff matrices and equilibrium conditions showing that, under reasonable parameter values (punishment not too costly, punishment more aversive than being cheated, etc.), punishment enables efficient outcomes. The analysis also shows that if punishment is too costly or ineffective, cooperation and efficiency break down.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency, provided that punishment is not excessively costly and is sufficiently effective to deter cheating. The model clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost/impact structure of punishment and the strategic response of players. If the control (no-punishment) game is dominated by cheaters (low efficiency), introducing punishment—even by self-interested types—can shift the equilibrium to high efficiency. The paper's explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions can be used to inform quantitative predictions, especially regarding the importance of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the strategic incentives for cooperation.

- source: Zhao, Y. K., Zhao, X. B., & Shen, Z. J. M. (2018). The hot-versus-cold effect in a punishment game: a multi-round experimental study. *Annals of Operations Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two elicitation methods (strategy vs. direct-response) in a multi-round, two-player punishment game. The strategy method leads to significantly higher punishment rates (84% vs. 62%) and more frequent cooperative sender actions (S1: 68% vs. 44%). Combined payoffs (group welfare) are higher for S1 actions, and the higher frequency of S1 under the strategy method results in higher average group payoffs. Regression and learning model analyses show that the difference in punishment rates is due to mental accounting: the strategy method allows receivers to integrate the cost and expected benefit of punishment, increasing its perceived utility. Over repeated rounds, senders learn to avoid punishment by choosing S1 more often, further increasing group welfare. Reward rates are low and do not differ between methods. The findings suggest that, in repeated games, the strategic value of punishment (deterrence) is more important than the immediate emotional satisfaction of punishing, and that the design of the punishment mechanism (including decision timing) can significantly affect cooperation and group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player punishment games, higher punishment rates (enabled by the strategy method) lead to more cooperative sender behavior and higher group payoffs. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the structure is adjacent: one player can take a selfish or cooperative action, and the other can punish. The results suggest that, in repeated settings, enabling effective punishment (with high perceived strategic value) increases efficiency by deterring selfish behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that, all else equal, punishment mechanisms that increase the perceived strategic value of punishment (e.g., via decision timing or repeated interaction) will lead to higher efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low due to frequent selfish actions. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, punishment-enabled games, but the mechanism may generalize to other repeated social dilemmas with punishment.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Third-party punishment and social norms. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that third-party punishment is common and increases with the severity of norm violations in both dictator games (distribution norm) and prisoner's dilemma games (cooperation norm). However, the magnitude of third-party punishment is lower than that of second-party punishment. In dictator games, third-party punishment reduces but does not eliminate the profitability of unfair transfers, while second-party punishment makes unfair transfers unprofitable. In prisoner's dilemma games, third-party punishment reduces the gains from defection but does not make cooperation strictly dominant, whereas second-party punishment does. Thus, punishment mechanisms—especially when strong or when multiple punishers are present—can increase efficiency by reducing the incentive to violate norms, but the effect is stronger for second-party punishment. The paper does not directly report group efficiency as a percentage of the maximum, but provides enough data to infer that punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes by reducing the profitability of norm violations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (especially second-party, but also third-party) increases the cost of norm violations and can shift incentives toward more efficient, norm-compliant outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (with sufficient strength or multiple punishers) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to control conditions without punishment, but the effect size depends on the punishment's strength and whether it is second- or third-party. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the punishment cost/impact ratio, the number of punishers, and whether punishment is second- or third-party. The results are most directly applicable to dictator and prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to but not exact public goods games. The paper does not provide direct evidence for standard PGGs, but the mechanisms and patterns are likely to generalize to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures.

- source: dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Explaining the evolution of parochial punishment in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment can promote cooperation and increase group average payoff (efficiency) when it is targeted at cheats and used to enforce cooperation. However, the evolution of parochial punishment (harsher punishment of out-group cheats) depends on the ecological scenario. Parochial punishment evolves (and can support high between-group cooperation and group payoff) when groups interact collaboratively and conflict occurs with random groups, or when cooperation is difficult to enforce. In contrast, when conflict occurs with the same group as prior interactions, punishment becomes spiteful (targeting all out-groups, including cooperators), which undermines between-group cooperation and does not improve efficiency. The model shows that both collaborative intergroup relations and the structure of conflict are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent: it can be positive (by promoting cooperation) or negative (by reducing group payoff through excessive or misdirected punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games, especially in settings with group structure, third-party punishment, and group-level selection. It shows that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether punishment is targeted at cheats (promoting cooperation and group payoff) or is spiteful (reducing group payoff by punishing all out-groups). The presence and structure of intergroup conflict, the difficulty of enforcing cooperation, and the ability to form collaborative intergroup relationships are key moderators. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when it is used to enforce cooperation (especially when cooperation is hard to sustain), but can reduce efficiency if it is misdirected or used spitefully. The findings are most relevant for games with group structure, repeated interactions, and third-party punishment, but less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without these features.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cooperation Via Intimidation: An Emergent System of Mutual Threats can Maintain Social Order. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with post-game fighting (punishment), aggressive strategies (harassers) become evolutionarily stable and promote high rates of mutual cooperation (CC) in small groups with strong deterrence. The mechanism is that agents develop reputations for toughness, deterring defection and reducing the need for actual fighting. In large groups, anonymity undermines this effect, and cooperation rates fall. When third-party (moralized) punishment is allowed, coalitions can enforce cooperation, but only under certain conditions (small groups, justified defection rules). The models suggest that punishment can increase group-level cooperation and, by proxy, efficiency, but only under specific structural conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that enabling punishment in repeated PD-like environments can increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) compared to control, especially in small groups with strong deterrence and information spread. The effect is weaker or absent in large, anonymous groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is reputation-based and where agents can observe and remember others' aggressive acts. The results are not from public goods games but from repeated PDs with post-game punishment, so transfer to PGGs should be cautious but is likely informative for adjacent settings. The paper does not report efficiency as a direct ratio of group payoff to maximum, but CC rates can be used as a proxy for high group welfare.

- source: Hernandez, I., Cohen, D., Gruschow, K., Nowak, A., Gelfand, M. J., & Borkowski, W. (2022). The Importance of Being Unearnest: Opportunists and the Making of Culture. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that (1) more severe punishment for noncooperation reduces the amount of voluntary cooperation in a population, both in agent-based simulations and in a human experiment; (2) raising the standard for what counts as sufficient cooperation to avoid punishment also reduces voluntary cooperation; (3) the presence of opportunists is necessary for strong reciprocators to thrive, but when punishment is too severe, opportunists proliferate and strong reciprocators decline; (4) these dynamics help explain why tight or collectivist cultures with strong norms and sanctions may have lower social capital (voluntary cooperation) than expected. The main outcome is the amount of voluntary cooperation, not direct group efficiency or total payoff, but the results imply that harsher punishment can reduce overall welfare by crowding out voluntary, uncoerced cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with punishment, increasing the severity of punishment or raising the bar for what counts as sufficient cooperation can reduce the amount of voluntary cooperation, and thus likely reduce group efficiency or welfare. The presence of punishment is necessary for sustaining cooperation, but making punishment too severe or standards too high backfires. For prediction tasks, this suggests a non-monotonic or negative effect of increasing punishment severity on efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or standards are high. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible payoffs, so the mapping to efficiency is indirect. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of punishment severity and standards on voluntary cooperation and, by extension, on group efficiency in similar multi-agent, multi-round games.

- source: Boyd, R., & Mathew, S. (2015). Third-party monitoring and sanctions aid the evolution of language. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper develops a repeated signaling game (a variant of the Sir Philip Sidney game) to model the evolution of honest communication. It finds that when only direct reciprocity is possible and lies are hard to detect, honest signaling is not evolutionarily stable except in special cases. However, when third-party monitoring and punishment are introduced—so that multiple community members can observe, aggregate evidence, and sanction dishonesty—the range of parameters supporting honest signaling expands greatly. This is because third-party monitoring increases the probability of detecting lies and reduces false accusations, making it more likely that group-beneficial honest signaling (analogous to high efficiency) is maintained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that adding third-party monitoring and punishment mechanisms to repeated social dilemmas (even when not strictly public goods games) can substantially increase the likelihood of group-beneficial outcomes, such as honest signaling or cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of third-party punishment and monitoring should be expected to have a strongly positive effect on efficiency or group payoff, especially when direct detection of defection is difficult. The model is not a standard PGG, but the logic is closely related and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where monitoring and punishment are possible.

- source: SCHMITT, D. R., & MARWELL, G. (1970). REWARD AND PUNISHMENT AS INFLUENCE TECHNIQUES FOR ACHIEVEMENT OF COOPERATION UNDER INEQUITY. *Human Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dyadic task with unequal cooperative payoffs, the ability to reward (transfer money) is much more effective than the ability to punish (subtract money) in inducing and sustaining high rates of cooperation. Groups where the reward strategy was used spent more than twice as much time cooperating compared to those using punishment or no strategy. Communication between subjects further increased the use of rewards and the likelihood of achieving steady-state cooperation, particularly among males. Punishment, when used alone, was generally ineffective in producing cooperation, and its use declined over time. The study does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total earnings, but the behavioral outcomes suggest that reward-based influence is more conducive to sustained cooperation than punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment and reward on cooperation in a setting adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment alone is generally ineffective at inducing sustained cooperation, while reward (and especially the combination of reward and communication) is much more effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is dyadic, with asymmetric payoffs and the ability for only one player to reward or punish, which differs from standard PGGs. Thus, the main value for prediction is as a caution that punishment may not always increase cooperation or efficiency, especially when reward is also available and more salient.

- source: Whitson, J. A., Wang, C. S., See, Y. H. M., Baker, W. E., & Murnighan, J. K. (2015). How, when, and why recipients and observers reward good deeds and punish bad deeds. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party observers are more likely to punish bad deeds (e.g., deception) than direct recipients, and that direct recipients are more likely to reward good deeds (e.g., honesty) than observers. These effects are mediated by feelings of obligation: observers feel a stronger obligation to punish, while recipients feel a stronger obligation to reward. The studies use one-shot, controlled scenarios with real monetary stakes, but do not involve repeated interaction, group provision, or public goods. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward assigned), not group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in one-shot, dyadic or triadic settings, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that the presence of observers may increase the likelihood and intensity of punishment for norm violations, but do not inform how this affects group payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about who is likely to punish and under what circumstances, not about the payoff consequences of punishment.

- source: Guo, Y. C., Zhao, X. R., Liu, Y. B., & Ma, J. H. (2024). The effect of third-party intervention on normative behavior of bystanders: An explanation of the social norm cueing effect. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both third-party punishment and third-party compensation, when observed by bystanders, increase the bystanders' fairness in subsequent dictator game allocations. This effect is mediated by changes in descriptive norm perceptions. The effect is present when the intervention is presented as group summary information (Study 1) or as an individual action perceived as representative of group behavior (Study 2). The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a dictator game, not a public goods game or its direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that observing third-party punishment or compensation can increase norm-compliant behavior (fairness in allocation) among bystanders, mediated by changes in perceived descriptive norms. However, because the study uses a dictator game (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding norm transmission and the psychological mechanisms by which punishment or compensation can affect behavior, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Böckler, A., Tusche, A., & Singer, T. (2016). The Structure of Human Prosociality: Differentiating Altruistically Motivated, Norm Motivated, Strategically Motivated, and Self-Reported Prosocial Behavior. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial behavior is multidimensional, with four robust factors: altruistically motivated, norm motivated (including punishment and reciprocity), strategically motivated, and self-reported prosociality. Punishment behavior (in second and third person punishment games) loads on the 'norm motivated' factor, indicating its role in norm enforcement. The study replicates standard findings in economic games (e.g., people punish unfairness, give more in dictator than in punishment games, help more when reciprocated), but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The factor structure is stable across two large samples. Altruistic prosociality is associated with lower negative affect and higher cognitive skills. Gender, age, and income show differential associations with the factors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment behavior is a distinct, norm-motivated component of prosociality, separable from altruistic and strategic motivations. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design details of the punishment games (one-shot, anonymous, fixed punishment effectiveness) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task. The study is most useful for understanding the motivational structure underlying punishment and cooperation, not for quantifying efficiency effects.

- source: Falk, A., Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2005). Driving forces behind informal sanctions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a three-player one-shot PD with punishment, cooperators predominantly punish defectors, and this pattern is robust across different punishment effectiveness conditions and elicitation methods. Defectors also punish, but their punishment is more likely to be spiteful and disappears when punishment cannot increase payoff differences. The cost-effectiveness of punishment is manipulated, showing that cooperators are willing to spend more to punish defectors even when punishment is less effective. The results challenge fairness theories that focus only on group-level payoffs or inequality aversion, supporting instead a model where retaliation against unfairness is central. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and motivational aspects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on the behavioral patterns and motives of punishment in a three-player PD with punishment, including how punishment is targeted and how its cost-effectiveness affects behavior. However, it does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that punishment is frequently used by cooperators against defectors, and that the willingness to punish persists even when it is costly, which may imply potential for efficiency improvements in similar games, but this is not tested or quantified here. The design details (player count, one-shot, punishment cost/effectiveness) are well specified and can inform mechanistic expectations, but not direct efficiency predictions.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Schiller, B., Rieskamp, J., Gianotti, L. R., & Knoch, D. (2014). Diminishing parochialism in intergroup conflict by disrupting the right temporo-parietal junction. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is more severe toward outgroup norm violators than ingroup violators (parochial punishment), especially in cases where the outgroup member defects against an ingroup member. Disrupting the right TPJ with rTMS reduces this parochial punishment, making third-parties treat outgroup and ingroup violators more similarly. This effect is mediated by a reduction in the motive to retaliate and a reduction in mentalizing bias (the tendency to take the perspective of ingroup more than outgroup members). The manipulation does not affect punishment in intragroup constellations or in non-defection cases. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying parochial punishment in third-party punishment games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a Prisoner's Dilemma for norm violation and a third-party punishment stage, but does not involve repeated public goods contributions or measure group efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why punishment is more severe toward outgroup members, and how neural interventions can reduce this bias. However, the results cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no efficiency or payoff data are reported. The punishment technology (1 point spent reduces target's payoff by 3) is specified, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned) and motivational (retaliation, perspective-taking), not payoff-based.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Özdemir, Y., & Fehr, E. (2014). The Value of Vengeance and the Demand for Deterrence. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that both retributive and deterrence motives drive costly punishment: participants punish norm violators even when the punishment is hidden and cannot deter future violations, but punishment is stronger when it can be communicated. Second-party punishment (when the punisher is the direct victim) is stronger than third-party punishment (when the punisher is an observer). The findings suggest that retributive motives can lead to punishment that does not increase social welfare and may be inefficient. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be motivated by retribution alone, leading to costly punishment even when it cannot improve future cooperation or group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency or group payoff, especially when punishment is driven by retributive motives rather than deterrence. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games, using a trust game with punishment, so findings may transfer in terms of understanding motives but not in terms of efficiency outcomes.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Rijkhoff, S. A. M., & van Dijk, E. (2013). Equality as a benchmark for third-party punishment and reward: The moderating role of uncertainty in social dilemmas. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishers and rewarders use the equality rule as a benchmark for sanctioning decisions only when the size of the common resource is certain. Under certainty, those who take more than an equal share are punished much more, and those who take an equal or smaller share are rewarded more. Under uncertainty, the amount of punishment or reward is linearly related to the amount harvested, with no sharp benchmark. The emotional basis for punishment is negative affect (anger), while for reward it is positive affect. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or common resource games, as it does not report any payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how the clarity of the cooperation norm (equality rule) and environmental uncertainty affect the application of punishment and reward. In settings where the cooperation norm is clear and known, punishment is more sharply targeted at norm violators; under uncertainty, punishment is more diffuse. This may indirectly inform predictions about the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation or efficiency, but the paper does not provide direct evidence for such effects.

- source: Guo, Z. Y., Guo, R., Xu, C. Y., & Wu, Z. (2022). Reflexive or reflective? Group bias in third-party punishment in Chinese and Western cultures. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both Chinese and Western participants show ingroup favoritism in third-party punishment under reflexive (fast, high cognitive load) conditions, punishing ingroup norm violators less than outgroup violators. Under reflective (slow, low cognitive load) conditions, Western participants—especially those with high group identity—reduce ingroup favoritism and may even punish ingroup violators more, while Chinese participants continue to show strong ingroup favoritism regardless of decision mode. Group identity amplifies these effects. The main outcome is the amount of punishment assigned, not efficiency or group payoff. The game is a third-party punishment mini-dictator game, not a standard public goods game, and does not report on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity, culture, and decision-making mode moderate the behavioral use of costly punishment in a third-party punishment context. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is adjacent to PGGs (mini-dictator/TPP), and the outcome is punishment behavior, not payoff. The findings are useful for understanding when and how punishment is used, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, X. H., Molleman, L., & van Dolder, D. (2021). Do descriptive social norms drive peer punishment? Conditional punishment strategies and their impact on cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that individuals' punishment of defectors is conditional on descriptive social norms: people punish more when cooperation or punishment is more common in a reference group. There is substantial heterogeneity: some punish independently, some increase punishment with higher cooperation (norm enforcement), and some with higher punishment (conformist). The dynamic model demonstrates that these conditional punishment strategies can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of high cooperation rates in populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment behavior is conditional on social context, and that such conditional punishment can promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. However, because the experiment is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with punishment and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games. The dynamic model suggests that conditional punishment can help sustain high cooperation rates, which may imply higher efficiency in analogous settings, but this is an indirect inference. The paper is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and heterogeneity in punishment, and for modeling the potential for punishment to support cooperation, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Kurzban, R., DeScioli, P., & O'Brien, E. (2007). Audience effects on moralistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
