The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Gillenkirch, R. M., & Kreienbaum, H. (2017). What guides subjective performance evaluation: Incentive alignment or norm enforcement?. *Review of Managerial Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that superiors are primarily motivated by norm enforcement: they pay more for information that allows them to reward high and punish low effort, and their allocation decisions reflect a willingness to punish norm violations. Subordinates increase their effort when they know their effort can be observed and evaluated. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total surplus, but higher effort levels in conditions with more informative evaluation suggest that group output and subordinate payoffs increase when norm enforcement is possible. However, the focus is on behavioral mechanisms (effort, information acquisition, allocation) rather than on efficiency as a formal outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling punishment (via subjective evaluation and information acquisition) increases effort and likely increases group output in a team production setting with a free rider problem. However, it does not report efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The design is adjacent to PGGs: two subordinates choose effort (costly), output is the sum of efforts plus noise, and a superior (not a group member) allocates the output. The 'punishment' is implemented via allocation decisions, not explicit costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that mechanisms enabling norm enforcement (punishment) can increase effort and likely improve group outcomes, but the lack of direct efficiency data and the non-standard game structure limit its direct applicability to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Garrett, J. B., Livingston, J. A., Tayler, W. B., Cade, N. L., & McVay, S. E. (2019). Controls and Cooperation in Interactive and Non-Interactive Settings. *Contemporary Accounting Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a control (audit with bonus) in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases subsequent cooperative behavior (measured as investment in a trust game) for both participants who directly experienced the control and those who only observed it, but the effect is significantly larger for those who directly benefited from cooperation. The effect is mediated by observed cooperation in the initial game. The results suggest that controls can foster trust and reciprocity, and that these effects are stronger in interactive settings. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the main outcome is behavioral (investment/cooperation), not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that control mechanisms (akin to monitoring or incentive-based controls) can increase cooperative behavior in subsequent social dilemmas, especially in interactive settings. However, because the main outcome is investment in a trust game (a behavioral measure) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not involve explicit punishment or sanctions, but rather a positive incentive (bonus for high contribution). The results suggest that mechanisms increasing observed cooperation can have positive spillovers on future cooperation, but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Kuwabara, K. (2015). Do Reputation Systems Undermine Trust? Divergent Effects of Enforcement Type on Generalized Trust and Trustworthiness. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Reputation systems, as a form of diffuse, non-material enforcement, reinforce generalized trust and trustworthiness more than contractual (material punishment) or relational (repeated partner) enforcement. After exposure to reputational enforcement, participants are more trusting and trustworthy in subsequent unenforced exchanges than after exposure to contracts or repeated partners. Contractual enforcement, in contrast, can undermine intrinsic motivation to trust or be trustworthy once enforcement is removed. These effects are robust across multiple studies and are supported by both behavioral and self-report measures. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the experimental paradigm is the investment game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like enforcement (contracts) and reputation systems on subsequent cooperation, but not on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that material punishment (contracts) can undermine intrinsic motivation for cooperation, while reputation systems can reinforce it. However, because the outcomes are trust/trustworthiness (not efficiency) and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The evidence is more relevant for understanding how enforcement mechanisms affect willingness to cooperate after enforcement is removed, rather than direct effects on group efficiency during enforcement.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Karagonlar, G., & Kuhlman, D. M. (2013). The role of Social Value Orientation in response to an unfair offer in the Ultimatum Game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the paper finds that Prosocials (as measured by SVO) are less likely to reject an unfair offer (20% of the pie) in the Ultimatum Game than Proselfs. This difference is not due to differences in fairness perceptions or expectations, but is mediated by more positive emotions and more positive attributions toward the proposer among Prosocials. The findings support the view that emotion regulation and cognitive reappraisal, rather than altruistic punishment, drive acceptance of unfair offers among Prosocials. The study does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The Ultimatum Game is structurally different from public goods games, and the outcome of interest is the accept/reject decision rather than group payoff or efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding individual differences in responses to norm violations and costly punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does, however, suggest that motivations for punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) may differ by individual orientation, which could be contextually relevant for interpreting punishment effects in other games.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Klapwijk, A., & Van Munster, L. M. (2011). How the shadow of the future might promote cooperation. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the mere anticipation of repeated interaction (10 rounds vs. 1 round) increases cooperation (measured as coins given to the partner) among individualists, but not among prosocials, in a sequential give-some dilemma. Prosocials maintain high cooperation regardless of repetition, while individualists reciprocate more when repeated interaction is expected. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of 'noise' in the translation of choices. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that repeated interaction (longer games) can increase cooperation among individualistically oriented participants, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. However, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or include punishment, so its relevance to the prediction of efficiency under punishment is limited. The main implication is that repeated interaction may substitute for some effects of punishment among individualists, but this is not tested in a payoff or efficiency framework.

- source: Vandermeer, J., Hosey, C., Epley, N., & Keysar, B. (2019). Escalation of negative social exchange: Reflexive punishment or deliberative deterrence?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative escalation in repeated taking/giving games is driven by reflexive punishment rather than strategic deterrence. Deliberation reduces escalation, while reflexive or high cognitive load conditions increase it. Escalation occurs only in response to perceived negative actions (taking), not positive ones (giving). Explicitly instructing participants to focus on future consequences or to deter does not increase escalation; only a goal to punish does. The findings are robust across one-shot and repeated interactions, and across different manipulations of deliberation and cognitive resources. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not implement a punishment stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is a repeated dictator/taking game, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are escalation and behavioral responses to harm, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no punishment stage where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs, and no analysis of group welfare or efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punitive escalation, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Liu, W. X., Wang, H., Zhu, H. J., Zhu, X. Y., He, X. Y., & Zhang, W. (2022). Morality is Supreme: The Roles of Morality, Fairness and Group Identity in the Ultimatum Paradigm. *Psychology Research and Behavior Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, responders are more likely to accept offers (including unfair ones) from proposers with positive moral backgrounds than from those with negative moral backgrounds. This effect is robust across fair, moderately unfair, and extremely unfair offers. Group identity, manipulated via moral priming, has only a partial effect: positive or negative moral priming increases acceptance rates for positive moral proposers in unfair allocations, but does not significantly affect responses to negative moral proposers. The gender of the proposer does not significantly affect acceptance rates. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are not directly transferable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in ultimatum game settings, the perceived morality of the proposer strongly influences acceptance rates, even for unfair offers. However, because the outcome measured is acceptance rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the ultimatum game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, costs, or efficiency, so it does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social/moral context in responder behavior, which may inform broader models of cooperation and norm enforcement.

- source: Schram, A., & Charness, G. (2015). Inducing Social Norms in Laboratory Allocation Choices. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that providing impartial advice about what one 'ought to do' (shared understanding) only affects behavior when choices are made public, i.e., when social (not just moral) norms are salient. In the advice + public condition, dictators are significantly less selfish than in other conditions. Advice alone (private) does not change behavior, nor does public observability alone without advice. The effect is interpreted as the creation of a social norm, enforced by the threat of social disapproval (shame), not by material punishment. There is no direct measurement or analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. The findings are relevant for understanding norm induction and the role of observability, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments. It does not implement punishment, rewards, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how social norms (via advice and observability) can shift behavior toward less selfish choices in allocation games. For prediction tasks, the main takeaway is that nonmaterial social sanctions (public observability) combined with shared understanding can induce prosocial behavior, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a PGG or punishment mechanism limit its direct applicability.

- source: Rand, D. G., Brescoll, V. L., Everett, J. A. C., Capraro, V., & Barcelo, H. (2016). Social Heuristics and Social Roles: Intuition Favors Altruism for Women but Not for Men. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuition (via time pressure, conceptual priming, or cognitive load) increases altruistic giving in the Dictator Game for women, but not for men. This effect is further moderated by gender role identification: women who identify with masculine attributes become less altruistic when deliberating, while those who identify with feminine attributes are unaffected by deliberation. Men are generally less altruistic, and their giving is not significantly affected by cognitive processing mode or gender role identification. The study does not involve strategic interaction, punishment, or group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is the Dictator Game, which is a unilateral giving game with no strategic interaction, no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and no group payoff or efficiency concept. While the paper discusses social norms and the internalization of altruism (and mentions that women are punished more for failing to be altruistic), it does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report any efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as very indirect context about gender, social heuristics, and norm compliance.

- source: Mellers, B. A., Haselhuhn, M. P., Tetlock, P. E., Silva, J. C., & Isen, A. M. (2010). Predicting Behavior in Economic Games by Looking Through the Eyes of the Players. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Players' self-defined want and ought motives predict their offers in ultimatum and dictator games; (2) in the ultimatum game, more players act according to what they believe is fair (ought), while in the dictator game, want and ought are equally influential; (3) positive mood induction increases the likelihood of making equal offers in the dictator game; (4) when given an exit option, players experiencing greater conflict between want and ought are more likely to exit. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in public goods or repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment institutions, marginal per-capita return, or group efficiency. Its findings on rejections in the ultimatum game and exit behavior in the dictator game are only loosely related to the prediction task. The main value is in highlighting the psychological mechanisms (want/ought selves) that may underlie cooperation and sanctioning, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions.

- source: Testori, M., Giardini, F., Cruz, T. D. D., & Beersma, B. (2023). What Matters Most in Supporting Cooperation, the Gossip Content or the Gossiper's Intention? Simulating Motive Interpretation in Gossip Dynamics. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on how agents interpret the motives behind gossip and how they update reputations. If gossip is interpreted as pro-social and agents use a single reputation system, cooperation can rise to high levels. If gossip is interpreted as selfish or negative, a double reputation system (where agents discount unreliable gossip but do not punish the gossiper by refusing to cooperate) helps maintain initial cooperation levels. The model highlights the importance of motive interpretation and the structure of reputation systems for sustaining cooperation, but does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it offers indirect insights into how information transmission, reputation updating, and the interpretation of motives can moderate the effects of social information on cooperation. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which allow agents to discount unreliable information without ostracizing the source may help sustain cooperation, but the model's outcomes are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Shutters, S. T., & Hales, D. (2013). Tag-Mediated Altruism is Contingent on How Cheaters Are Defined. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the sustainability of tag-mediated altruism is highly sensitive to how cheaters are defined. When cheaters are defined as agents who can never donate (strong cheaters), altruism collapses completely and rapidly. With medium cheaters (who can revert to altruism via mutation), donation rates are very low. Only with ultraweak or weak cheaters do substantial donation rates persist. The paper also finds that parameters such as neighborhood size, number of pairings, and mutation rate affect donation rates, but the presence of strong cheaters overwhelms these effects. The authors suggest that additional mechanisms, such as punishment or multi-level selection, may be necessary to sustain cooperation in the presence of strong cheaters, but these are not modeled directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. It does not implement punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative in showing that, in tag-based donation models, the presence of strong cheaters (analogous to unpunished defectors) eliminates cooperation, and that additional mechanisms (such as punishment) may be required to sustain altruism. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that in environments where strong, unpunished defection is possible, efficiency will be low unless additional mechanisms (like punishment) are present. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct manipulation of punishment, so its value for prediction is mainly conceptual and indirect.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, Z. S., Krumhuber, E. G., & Chen, H. (2021). Money and flexible generosity. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the authors find that using money as the currency in DG/UG games reduces generosity compared to candy or food, but only when there are no sanctions (DG) or reputation concerns (private condition). When punishment is possible (UG) or when allocations are public (reputation at stake), the negative effect of money on generosity is reduced or eliminated. Imbuing money with prosocial meaning (charity) also attenuates the effect. The main outcome is generosity rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of sanctions or reputation mechanisms can offset the self-serving effects of money on prosocial behavior, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment or reputation mechanisms can mitigate the negative effect of monetary incentives on prosocial behavior, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. The results are most relevant for predicting how punishment or reputation might moderate the effect of monetary incentives on generosity or cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that punishment/reputation mechanisms may help maintain prosocial behavior even in monetary contexts, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Azevedo, R. T., Panasiti, M. S., Maglio, R., & Aglioti, S. M. (2018). Perceived warmth and competence of others shape voluntary deceptive behaviour in a morally relevant setting. *British Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the perceived warmth and competence of the target modulate the likelihood of lying for self-gain: participants lie less to high-warmth individuals and more to high-competence individuals. The risk of disclosure (reputation risk) reduces lying, particularly toward low-warmth/low-competence targets. These effects are interpreted as reflecting malleable personal moral standards and social evaluation processes. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the game is not a public goods game or direct variant. The outcomes are behavioral (lying frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social context, reputation risk, and target characteristics can modulate dishonest behavior in a zero-sum, two-player setting. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The manipulation of reputation risk is conceptually adjacent to punishment/sanction mechanisms, but there is no explicit punishment, and the outcome is not efficiency. Thus, the paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it may inform understanding of how social evaluation and disclosure risk affect norm-violating behavior.

- source: Gomes, C. M., Mulhinch, M. R., & McCullough, M. E. (2025). People decide whether to share social information-and whether to share it accurately-on the basis of self-interest. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that people are more likely to share reputational information (gossip) when they have behaved generously in a prior economic game, and less likely when they have behaved greedily. The tendency to share information honestly or dishonestly is strongly influenced by whether the participant stands to benefit from being chosen as a partner for a subsequent economic game. When lying is possible and being chosen is incentivized, greedy or indifferent participants are more likely to share dishonest, self-serving gossip. Generous participants are more likely to share honest information, especially when honesty is required and being chosen is incentivized. The results support reputation-based partner choice and social competition theories over indirect reciprocity theory. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it offers informative context about the strategic use of social information (gossip) in economic games with partner choice and reputation. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of information-sharing mechanisms (and their honesty) depends on participants' incentives and prior behavior, which may be relevant for understanding the role of reputation and information in environments with punishment or partner choice. Since no efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment, but may inform mechanistic expectations about when information-based interventions (including punishment or reputation) are likely to be effective or undermined by dishonesty.

- source: BIEL, A., & GARLING, T. (1995). THE ROLE OF UNCERTAINTY IN RESOURCE DILEMMAS. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The main finding is that a reputation mechanism that rewards/punishes based on the number of consecutive rounds a player has cooperated or defected can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. Higher reputation factors and higher upper bounds on consistent strategy times both further promote cooperation. The mechanism is more effective and leads to faster stabilization of cooperation than traditional reputation mechanisms. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments, and that the strength and structure of the reputation mechanism matter. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its relevance is further limited by the use of a spatial PDG rather than a PGG or direct payoff-based analysis. It may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Simpson, B., Harrell, A., & Willer, R. (2013). Hidden Paths from Morality to Cooperation: Moral Judgments Promote Trust and Trustworthiness. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, the authors show that (1) making moral judgments of others' immoral behavior increases subsequent trustworthiness in a trust game (measured by amount returned), (2) this effect is mediated by increased self-perceived moral identity, and (3) observers trust and prefer to interact with strong moral judges, perceiving them as more moral and trustworthy. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level trust and trustworthiness, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The design does not include public goods games, explicit punishment, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that making moral judgments (not material punishment) can increase trustworthiness in trust games, and that observers trust moral judges more. However, there is no manipulation of punishment, no public goods game, and no group payoff or efficiency outcome. The results may be relevant for understanding how non-material social judgments can affect prosocial behavior, but they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Yan, M. H., Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2023). Doing what others do does not stabilize continuous norms. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in models where norm-relevant behaviors are continuous (e.g., contribution levels in a public goods context), similarity-based payoffs (including social sanctions or punishment for deviating from the mean) do not stabilize arbitrary or costly norms. Instead, the equilibrium norm is determined by direct effects (such as individual or group payoffs, moral preferences, or cognitive/cultural attractors). Only in discrete norm spaces, or when categorization or multiple cognitive attractors are present, can similarity-based payoffs maintain multiple stable equilibria or persistent between-group differences. For public goods-like environments, this suggests that punishment or social sanctions alone are insufficient to maintain high-cooperation norms unless additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, discrete categories, or multiple optima) are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in continuous public goods games or similar environments, enabling punishment or social sanctions (modeled as similarity-based payoffs) will not, by itself, maintain high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibria. The equilibrium will be determined by direct payoff incentives, not by the presence of punishment, unless the game is discretized or there are additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, cognitive attractors) that create multiple optima. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in continuous-choice PGGs, the presence of punishment may not increase efficiency unless the design includes discrete choices, strong moral framing, or other mechanisms that create stable high-cooperation equilibria. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, but it strongly suggests that punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains in continuous settings.

- source: Boyd, R., & Richerson, P. J. (2022). Large-scale cooperation in small-scale foraging societies. *Evolutionary Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that large-scale cooperation (hundreds of individuals) in the production of public goods (e.g., communal hunts, construction of drivelines, warfare, habitat improvement) was common in Holocene and likely Pleistocene foraging societies, even at low population densities. Mechanisms such as direct sanctions (ostracism, denial of aid, physical punishment), social norms, and leadership roles were important for sustaining cooperation beyond the scale where mutualistic or reciprocal incentives suffice. The evidence challenges the view that ancestral human cooperation was limited to small groups. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that would directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides contextual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment and social sanctions can sustain large-scale cooperation in collective action problems, especially as group size increases and individual incentives to free-ride grow. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, MPCR, player count) on efficiency. Thus, while it supports the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in large groups, it does not provide data that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like experiments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of sanctions and institutions for large-scale cooperation and the likely need for additional mechanisms as group size increases.

- source: Li, T., Li, S. Q., Li, B. J., Zhang, Z. X., Luo, Y. J., & Feng, C. L. (2023). Navigating uncertainty in human social decision-making: Consequences and coping strategies. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The review finds that both social and nonsocial uncertainty can reduce prosocial behaviors by providing excuses for selfishness or increasing loss aversion, but in some contexts, uncertainty can also increase prosociality (e.g., by promoting forgiveness or overestimating one's impact on others). Coping strategies for social uncertainty include impression formation, impression updating, and compliance with social norms. Punishment and reward are discussed as norm enforcement tools, but the review does not analyze their effects on efficiency or group payoff. The review highlights the complexity and context-dependence of uncertainty's effects on cooperation and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual background on how uncertainty, punishment, and social norms interact in social decision-making games, including public goods and related environments. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding mechanisms and generating hypotheses about the role of uncertainty and norm enforcement.

- source: Jordan, J. J. (2023). A pull versus push framework for reputation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that reputation systems can incentivize cooperation through two distinct mechanisms: 'pull' (where evaluators are motivated to cooperate with good actors for direct reciprocal benefit) and 'push' (where evaluators are motivated by normative pressure to cooperate with good actors to maintain their own reputation). It reviews how these mechanisms map onto different game-theoretic models (e.g., coordination games, indirect reciprocity) and discusses their implications for psychology, behavior, and the design of reputation-based interventions. The framework is used to interpret existing empirical findings and to generate predictions about when and how reputation will promote cooperation or punishment, but does not test these predictions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers a conceptual framework that may help interpret when and why reputation, cooperation, and punishment mechanisms (including those in PGGs) are likely to be effective, depending on whether incentives are primarily 'pull' or 'push'. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the structure of the game (e.g., whether it is a coordination game, whether social norms are salient, whether actions are observable) will moderate the effectiveness of reputation and punishment, but does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The review finds that people are generally accurate in using behavioral and situational cues (such as past cooperation, communication, and game structure) to predict others' cooperativeness, but often misperceive the predictive value of personal cues (such as gender or attractiveness). Punishment is discussed as a behavioral cue, but its role is primarily in signaling trustworthiness rather than as a mechanism for increasing efficiency or group payoff. The review highlights that communication and increased mutual benefit (e.g., higher returns to cooperation) are situational factors that increase both actual and expected cooperation. However, the review does not provide quantitative or systematic evidence on how these factors affect efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.

However, the review does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions (such as punishment cost, MPCR, or player count) on efficiency. In contrast, humans possess specialized cognitive mechanisms (e.g., cheater detection, norm enforcement) that support stable cooperation and the use of punishment. The review highlights that while punishment is a common feature in human cooperation, it is much less prevalent in animal societies. The paper does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the behavioral and cognitive prerequisites for cooperation and punishment.

- source: Adam, T. C. (2010). Competition encourages cooperation: client fish receive higher-quality service when cleaner fish compete. *Animal Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Clients (ornate butterflyfish) with access to multiple cleaner fish stations receive higher-quality service from cleaners: they are cleaned for longer per event, are less likely to be ignored, and have fewer interactions terminated by the cleaner. However, the frequency of cheating by cleaners does not decrease with increased partner choice. Aggressive punishment (chasing) by clients in response to cheating is common and does not differ significantly between clients with and without partner choice. The study supports the role of partner choice (competition among cleaners) in promoting cooperative service, but suggests that punishment is used as a fixed strategy rather than being contingent on partner choice. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task: it shows that increased partner choice (analogous to increased competition or exit options in PGG-like games) leads to higher-quality cooperative behavior (service) from the provider, but does not reduce cheating frequency. Punishment (aggression) is used regardless of partner choice. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment costs or technology, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms (partner choice, punishment) are relevant, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The main value is in supporting the importance of partner choice and the robustness of punishment as a behavioral response.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2021). Cooperation in large-scale human societies-What, if anything, makes it unique, and how did it evolve?. *Evolutionary Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/A | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the key to sustaining cooperation in large-scale societies is the development of new institutional rules (social interaction mechanisms) that make prosocial behavior payoff-compatible for self-interested individuals, rather than a shift to decision-making mechanisms that are insensitive to payoff (e.g., strong conformity or prestige bias). It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that, in both small- and large-scale societies, humans are generally sensitive to material payoffs in social dilemmas, and that institutions such as monitoring, sanctioning, and reputation systems evolve to support cooperation. The paper highlights the importance of distinguishing between cooperative (payoff-increasing for all) and altruistic (payoff-decreasing for the actor) prosocial behaviors, and suggests that in large-scale societies, cooperation among non-relatives is typically cooperative, not altruistic, due to institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that the effect of punishment and institutional rules on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on whether those rules make cooperation payoff-compatible for self-interested agents. It suggests that, in predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, one should focus on the design of the social interaction mechanism (e.g., the structure and effectiveness of punishment, monitoring, and reward systems) and the degree to which these mechanisms align individual incentives with group welfare. However, the paper does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative relationships between game design dimensions and efficiency outcomes. Its value is in framing the importance of institutional design and payoff-sensitivity, rather than in providing parameter-specific predictions.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Cook, K. S., & Watabe, M. (1998). Uncertainty, trust, and commitment formation in the United States and Japan. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that when social uncertainty is high (i.e., risk of being exploited is greater), participants are more likely to form committed, stable relationships with specific partners, rather than seeking new partners or maximizing short-term gains. This effect is robust across both US and Japanese samples, and is stronger among individuals with lower general trust. The presence or absence of opportunities for exploitation (extortion/appropriation) is manipulated, but the main outcome is the frequency of commitment formation, not efficiency or group payoff. The results support the theory that social uncertainty drives commitment formation, and that general trust 'emancipates' individuals from closed, committed relationships. However, the study does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in environments with higher social uncertainty (risk of exploitation), individuals are more likely to form stable, committed relationships, which may facilitate cooperation. However, the experimental design does not include a public goods game, nor does it measure group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome. The 'punishment' or 'sanction' mechanism is limited to opportunities for exploitation (negative actions), not costly punishment of free riders. Therefore, while the findings may inform understanding of how uncertainty and trust affect partner choice and commitment, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The paper is best used as contextual or indirect evidence about the role of uncertainty and trust in cooperation and commitment, rather than as a source of quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kaltwasser, L., Hildebrandt, A., Wilhelm, O., & Sommer, W. (2016). Behavioral and neuronal determinants of negative reciprocity in the ultimatum game. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.

  findings: The study finds that both prosociality (as measured by social value orientation) and assertiveness predict the likelihood of rejecting unfair offers in the ultimatum game, supporting both the strong reciprocity and status defense models of negative reciprocity. Neural data (feedback negativity) show that individuals with higher negative reciprocity have stronger neural responses to proposer fairness. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. The findings are limited to the behavioral and neural correlates of negative reciprocity in the UG, not to efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is an ultimatum game, not a public goods game, and the main outcome is the rejection rate of unfair offers (a form of costly punishment), not group payoff or efficiency. While the study is relevant for understanding individual motivations for costly punishment (negative reciprocity), it does not inform how punishment affects group efficiency or payoff under different game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be weighted strongly for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, O., Shi, Y., & Li, K. (2025). Red, rather than blue can promote fairness in decision-making. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the color red (vs. blue) increases (vs. decreases) the amount offered by proposers in the ultimatum game, and this effect is mediated by perceived competitiveness. In the impunity game, where responders cannot punish proposers by rejecting offers, color has no effect on offers. The results suggest that contextual cues like color can influence fair behavior in allocation games, but only when strategic motives (i.e., the threat of rejection/punishment) are present. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not use a public goods game or direct punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers indirect evidence that contextual cues (like color) can affect proposer behavior in allocation games when strategic punishment is possible (as in the UG), but not when it is absent (as in the IG). The findings suggest that the presence or absence of punishment mechanisms (or their analogs) can moderate the effect of contextual factors on fair behavior, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Ellingsen, T., & Johannesson, M. (2008). Anticipated verbal feedback induces altruistic behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The opportunity for recipients to send anonymous verbal feedback (messages) to dividers in a one-shot dictator game significantly increases the average donation from 24.8% to 34.1% of the endowment. The fraction of zero donations drops from 42% to 21%, and equal splits rise from 30% to 48%. Negative feedback is almost always given when no money is received, and positive feedback is almost always given for equal splits. The results suggest that anticipated symbolic rewards and punishments (praise/blame) can promote altruistic behavior, even in anonymous, one-shot settings. However, the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the design is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that anticipated symbolic sanctions (verbal feedback) can increase prosocial behavior in allocation games, suggesting that non-material punishment/reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation or generosity. However, because the study uses a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated public goods game), does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the 'punishment' is symbolic rather than material, its direct predictive value for efficiency in public goods games with material punishment is limited. The findings may be most relevant for predicting the effect of adding symbolic feedback or communication channels, rather than material punishment, in similar environments.

The ability to overcome intuition (measured by CRT) increases the likelihood of correctly identifying pro-social motives. The study uses a one-shot dictator game with no communication, no punishment or reward mechanisms, and no group-level payoff structure. - source: Singh, M., & Garfield, Z. H. (2022). Evidence for third-party mediation but not punishment in Mentawai justice. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in the Mentawai context, third-party punishment is absent: no cases were found where third parties (outside the victim, their close kin, or spouse) directly punished or demanded penalties from transgressors. Indirect or second-order sanctions by third parties are also not observed. Instead, penalties (tulou) are negotiated and paid primarily to restore dyadic cooperation, with kinship playing a major role in the likelihood and size of payments. Mediation by third parties (especially shamans and elders) is common, particularly in more severe or between-clan disputes, but the mediator's role is to facilitate negotiation, not to enforce punishment. The study suggests that, in this small-scale society, institutionalized penalties serve to repair relationships rather than to enforce group-level norms through punishment. The findings are based on retrospective interviews and ethnographic observation of 302 reliable cases of transgression and response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The studied context is not a PGG or close variant, and the outcomes are not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in small-scale, kin-based societies, third-party punishment is rare or absent, and penalties serve to restore dyadic cooperation rather than to enforce group norms or increase group efficiency. If extrapolating to PGG-like settings, the results would suggest that the presence or absence of third-party punishment may depend on the social structure and the nature of cooperation (private vs. public goods), but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Thus, it is of limited use for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that punishment mechanisms may not always function as assumed in small-scale, kin-based societies.

- source: Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., Guemo, M., Robertson, T. E., & Tooby, J. (2012). The Psychosemantics of Free Riding: Dissecting the Architecture of a Moral Concept. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that people spontaneously and robustly categorize others as free riders only when they perceive intentional, exploitive undercontribution, not merely low contribution or accidental failure. This categorization is specific and not explained by general tendencies to categorize, by return rate rules, or by a general moral violator rule. Free riders elicit more punitive sentiment and are less preferred as cooperative partners, but these are attitudinal outcomes, not measured payoffs. The study provides evidence for a specialized psychological mechanism for free rider detection, supporting evolutionary game theory predictions about the need to identify and sanction intentional exploiters in collective action. However, the study does not use actual public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment institutions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or related economic outcomes. Instead, it provides evidence that people are psychologically prepared to identify and punish intentional free riders, and that this categorization is specific and robust. For the prediction task, this suggests that punishment mechanisms may be more effective at targeting intentional exploiters than accidental undercontributors, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about efficiency effects. Its main value is in informing the psychological underpinnings of punishment and norm enforcement, not in providing payoff-based effect sizes or moderators.

- source: Price, M. E. (2006). Judgments about cooperators and freeriders on a Shuar work team: An evolutionary psychological perspective. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Shuar villagers overwhelmingly judge higher contributors to a collective work project more positively (more respect, less punishment) than lower contributors, regardless of how much the worker benefits from the project. When contribution is held constant and benefit varies, those who participate more in collective actions (mainly men) are more likely to favor 'altruists' (high contributors, low benefit) and disfavor 'freeriders' (low contributors, high benefit), while low-participation subjects (mainly women) tend to favor those who benefit more, regardless of contribution. The study does not measure actual group payoffs, efficiency, or the effects of real punishment or reward options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that social judgments (including support for punishment) are strongly influenced by perceived contribution, and that individuals who participate more in collective actions are more likely to support punishing freeriders and favoring cooperators. The findings suggest that in environments where social judgment or informal sanctions are possible, support for punishing low contributors may be widespread, especially among frequent cooperators. However, because the study does not implement real punishment, measure actual payoffs, or use a public goods game design, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited and indirect.

- source: Vonasch, A. J., Reynolds, T., Winegard, B. M., & Baumeister, R. F. (2018). Death Before Dishonor: Incurring Costs to Protect Moral Reputation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that people value their moral reputation extremely highly, often above wealth, success, or even life itself. In hypothetical scenarios, large proportions of participants preferred jail, amputation, or death to acquiring a bad reputation. In lab experiments, many participants chose to endure pain or disgust (cold pressor, touching worms) rather than have information suggesting they were racist shared with others. The motivation to avoid reputation damage is strong and can drive costly behavior. However, the paper does not use public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment or sanctions in a game-theoretic sense, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, support the broader claim that reputation concerns can motivate costly prosocial or norm-compliant behavior, which is a mechanism often invoked in explaining why punishment can sustain cooperation. The findings may be useful for contextualizing why individuals might comply with norms or avoid free-riding, but they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effects of specific game design dimensions. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant and should not be weighted heavily for estimating efficiency outcomes.

- source: Voorhees, B., Read, D., & Gabora, L. (2020). Identity, Kinship, and the Evolution of Cooperation. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The authors propose that human cooperation is uniquely enabled by the evolution of cultural idea systems and kinship-based social identities, which prescribe cooperative behavior and motivate punishment of norm violators as a defense of social identity. They argue that this top-down, culturally mediated mechanism overcomes the second-order free rider problem and stabilizes cooperation in human groups. The paper does not present empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but offers a conceptual account of how cooperation and punishment are maintained in human societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. Its value for the prediction task is limited to high-level theoretical context: it suggests that the presence and strength of culturally mediated identities and norms may be important moderators of cooperation and punishment, but it does not specify how game design dimensions or control efficiency would quantitatively affect efficiency when punishment is enabled. The paper does not address or analyze any of the target game design dimensions in a way that would inform direct prediction.

- source: Hertz, U. (2024). A cognitive approach to learning, monitoring, and shifting social norms. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the cognitive demands of detecting and monitoring social norms vary by norm type: categorical norms are easiest to detect and punish, scale-sensitive norms require signal detection and are more ambiguous, and history-sensitive norms require tracking individual behavior over time. These differences affect the stability and flexibility of norms, the ease of norm change, and the likelihood of punishment. The paper references experimental paradigms such as the common-goods game and discusses how punishment likelihood increases with deviation from group norms, but does not report on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insights into how punishment and norm enforcement may operate differently depending on the cognitive demands of the norm type. It suggests that punishment is more likely and more effective for categorical norms, and that scale-sensitive and history-sensitive norms are more context-dependent and malleable. However, it does not provide empirical data or quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but may inform hypotheses about when punishment is likely to be more or less effective based on the type of norm and cognitive demands.

- source: Balliet, D., Molho, C., Columbus, S., & Cruz, T. D. D. (2022). Prosocial and punishment behaviors in everyday life. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial and punishment behaviors in daily life are highly context-dependent, varying with relationship quality, power dynamics, and the nature of the interaction. Direct punishment (as seen in lab games) is rare in daily life, especially among strangers; indirect forms like gossip and avoidance are more common. The review emphasizes that laboratory paradigms (such as public goods games with punishment) capture only a small subset of real-world social behavior, and that field methods reveal a broader range of strategies and contexts. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It is primarily a theoretical and methodological review, arguing that lab-based findings may not generalize to real-world contexts. The paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze the game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in highlighting the limitations of laboratory paradigms and the importance of context, but it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency effects from punishment in controlled games.

- source: Hart, E., Mellers, B. A., & Bicchieri, C. (2019). Bad luck or bad intentions: When do third parties reveal offenders' intentions to victims?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Third party observers are more likely to inform victims when harm is accidental than when it is intentional. Moral anger increases the likelihood of informing about intentional harm, while empathy increases the likelihood of informing in both cases. Observers are more likely to inform when they believe the victim's beliefs about the offender's intent are inaccurate, and when the information has consequences for future interactions (e.g., potential for punishment). However, the paper does not study or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods game environments. The design is adjacent in that it uses economic games and discusses punishment and fairness, but the dependent variables are about information sharing, not payoffs or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Kim, M., & Sivan, E. W. Z. (2017). Faking It Is Hard to Do: Entrepreneurial Norm Enforcement and Suspicions of Deviance. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when norm enforcement is mandated (i.e., actors are required to respond to deviance), enforcers are perceived as more sincere and less likely to be suspected of ulterior motives, even in high-accusability contexts. In contrast, when enforcement is entrepreneurial (i.e., voluntary and not mandated) and the context is high accusability (where actors have reason to fear being accused of deviance), enforcers are viewed with greater suspicion and are more likely to be seen as having ulterior motives. In low-accusability contexts, entrepreneurial enforcement does not arouse suspicion. The study does not measure or report on efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about social perceptions of norm enforcers, not about behavioral or payoff consequences of punishment. The design is adjacent to PGG-like environments in that it involves norm enforcement and public goods logic, but it is not an economic game and does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational moderators of norm enforcement.

- source: Benard, S., Doan, L., Nicholson, D. A., Meanwell, E., Wright, E. L., & Lista, P. (2024). An Eye For an Eye Versus Turning The Other Cheek? The Status Consequences of Revenge and Forgiveness in Intergroup Conflict. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Forgiveness in intergroup conflict is perceived as more status-worthy than revenge, across national, political, and sports identities. (2) Justifying actions as group-motivated (versus individually motivated) has little effect on perceived status, except for a small positive effect for forgiveness in the sports scenario. (3) Open-ended responses show that forgiving actors are more often described as competent, warm, and high-status, while vengeful actors are more often described as dominant and conflict-seeking. (4) There is some evidence that group-motivated revenge is seen as making the group look bad. (5) The study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any material or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it conceptually links intergroup conflict to public goods problems and discusses status as a selective incentive, all outcomes are attitudinal (perceived status, trait attributions) rather than behavioral or payoff-based. There is no manipulation of actual contribution, punishment, or reward in a game-theoretic or economic sense, and no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Thus, the paper is not informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games with or without punishment.

The study conceptualizes pandemic-related prosocial behaviors as a social dilemma but does not use a public goods game or direct analog, nor does it measure or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. No experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions is present; support for behavioral regulations is measured as an attitude, not as an implemented or experienced sanctioning institution.
