- source: Roberts, G. (2020). Honest signaling of cooperative intentions. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that costly signaling of cooperative intent can evolve as an honest signal when the long-term benefits of repeated cooperation outweigh the costs of signaling. In such environments, agents who signal are more likely to be chosen as partners, and those who are chosen are more likely to cooperate, leading to higher payoffs for both signalers and choosers. The model predicts that efficiency (total payoff) is maximized when signaling costs are moderate and the number of repeated interactions is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and is based on dyadic rather than group interactions, but the findings are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can support efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms allowing individuals to signal cooperative intent and choose partners based on such signals can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the model is closely related and suggests that, in environments where reputation-building is possible and partner choice is allowed, efficiency will be higher than in environments without these mechanisms. The absence of punishment in the model means it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of costly signaling and partner choice as alternative or complementary mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling reputation-building and partner choice (analogous to some forms of communication or signaling in PGGs) can increase efficiency, especially when the cost of signaling is not prohibitive and the benefits of cooperation are high.

- source: Waldeck, R. (2013). Segregated Cooperation. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with internalized norm adherence (modeled as a private emotional cost for defecting), local interaction structures (small neighborhoods) can lead to persistent, segregated clusters of cooperators, resulting in higher average fitness (payoff) for cooperators compared to defectors. This effect is robust to various simulation parameters (error rate, number of active players, population size) but is highly sensitive to the distribution of emotional adherence and the cost of cooperation. When the basin of attraction for defection is large (e.g., high cost of cooperation or unfavorable emotion distribution), cooperation collapses even in local structures. No explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; all norm enforcement is via internalized emotion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or PD games, as it does not include any explicit punishment mechanism. Instead, it models norm adherence as an internalized emotional cost. The findings are relevant for understanding how local interaction structure and heterogeneity in norm adherence can affect efficiency and cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the efficiency impact of punishment interventions, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in environments with internalized norm enforcement and local interactions.

- source: Ohdaira, T., & Terano, T. (2011). Scale-Free Relationships Facilitate Cooperation in Spatial Games with Sequential Strategy. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial game with a 'second-best' decision rule, scale-free network structures facilitate the emergence and speed of mutual cooperation more than regular or random networks, especially when the strategy length (number of choices per match) is large. Reducing the number of strategies per group increases similarity and promotes early cooperation, but slows further evolution. The density of connections and the total number of groups have little effect. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are reported as frequencies of mutual cooperation/defection, not as efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Its main relevance is indirect: it shows that network topology (especially scale-free structure) can facilitate cooperation under a compromise-based decision rule, but this is not in a PGG or with punishment. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how network structure moderates cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Ocampo, J., & Keltner, D. (2024). Dispositional compassion shifts social preferences in systematic ways. *Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that people higher in dispositional compassion express less satisfaction with both advantageous and disadvantageous monetary allocations relative to others, indicating a preference for equality. These effects are robust to controls for prosociality, positive affect, and other personality traits. Experimental inductions of state compassion or pride do not reliably shift these social preferences. The tasks are abstract, one-shot, and hypothetical, with no real economic consequences or group interaction. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to economic games but lacks key features: there is no group interaction, no repeated rounds, no punishment or reward, and no real payoffs. The findings may inform how dispositional compassion relates to preferences for equality in allocation, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is very limited.

- source: Bravo, G. (2010). Voluntary contribution to public goods in mutual-help forums: reciprocity or group attachment?. *Socio-economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the success of the mutual-help forum depends on the complementary roles of a small core of altruistic contributors and a larger group of reciprocators. Reciprocity is a significant motivator for most users, while the core group is driven by intrinsic motivation and group attachment. The forum achieves high performance in terms of questions answered and response speed, but this is not analyzed in terms of efficiency or group payoff. There is no evidence of formal or informal punishment mechanisms affecting contribution or efficiency. The findings suggest that, in the absence of sanctioning, a privileged core group can sustain cooperation and public good provision in online communities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a real-world mutual-help forum without any explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how cooperation can be sustained without punishment, especially through reciprocity and intrinsic motivation. However, the absence of efficiency or payoff outcomes, and the lack of any punishment treatment, mean that this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful as contextual evidence for baseline cooperation in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Mifune, N., Hashimoto, H., & Yamagishi, T. (2010). Altruism toward in-group members as a reputation mechanism. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The presence of eye images (cues of monitoring) on participants' computer screens increased the frequency of fair (equal) allocations in a one-shot dictator game, but only when the recipient was an in-group member. No such effect was found for out-group recipients. The effect is interpreted as evidence that reputation concerns (triggered by monitoring cues) selectively enhance altruism toward in-group members. The study does not involve repeated interaction, public goods, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot dictator game with no punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the outcome is allocation behavior rather than group payoff or efficiency. The only relevant dimension is the effect of monitoring cues (eyes) on prosocial behavior, which may be loosely analogous to reputation or observability manipulations in public goods games, but not to punishment. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Ernest-Jones, M., Nettle, D., & Bateson, M. (2011). Effects of eye images on everyday cooperative behavior: a field experiment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Displaying images of eyes in a cafeteria significantly reduced the likelihood of littering (i.e., increased norm compliance/cooperation), independent of whether the accompanying message was relevant to littering. The effect was strongest when there were fewer people present (i.e., when natural cues of observation were sparse). The study provides evidence that subtle cues of being watched can promote cooperative behavior in real-world settings, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how cues of observation (such as eye images) can increase norm compliance in real-world settings, especially when actual observation is low. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, marginal per-capita return, or other core PGG design dimensions, nor does it report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of observation cues on cooperation, but not for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bernhard, R. M., & Cushman, F. (2022). Extortion, intuition, and the dark side of reciprocity. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that both communication and subsidies for NCH can shift groups toward more cooperative, higher-efficiency outcomes, but also that some groups coordinate on Nash equilibria (heavy spraying) that are also efficient in the game’s payoff structure. Group-level characteristics are more predictive of cooperative behavior than individual traits. The game does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no evidence is provided about the effects of punishment on efficiency or cooperation. The findings are most relevant for understanding the effects of communication and positive incentives (subsidies) on coordination and efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but not for punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It does provide informative evidence about the effects of communication and subsidies, and about the importance of group-level characteristics for coordination and efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks involving communication or positive incentives, the findings are more relevant.

- source: Robbins, B., Karell, D., Siegenthaler, S., & Kamm, A. (2023). Pathways to prosocial leadership: an online experiment on the effects of external subsidies and the relative price of giving. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: External subsidies (private piece-rate transfers) to leaders increase the proportion of community tokens given to recipients, thus increasing group welfare. The effect is robust to the timing of subsidy introduction or removal, with no evidence of crowding out or reduced giving after subsidy removal. The relative price of giving (multiplier) does not significantly affect giving. Norms about giving are not affected by either subsidies or the multiplier. The design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game, but the main outcome is directly related to group welfare/efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that external subsidies to leaders (akin to positive incentives or rewards) can increase group welfare in resource allocation settings, even when leaders have full discretion. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves redistribution rather than voluntary contribution to a public pool. The findings suggest that positive incentives can increase efficiency, but cannot be directly transferred to punishment effects. The absence of punishment, the dictator game structure, and the focus on leader-driven redistribution limit its direct applicability to PGG-like punishment predictions, but the evidence is informative for the effect of positive incentives on efficiency.

- source: Panchanathan, K., Frankenhuis, W. E., & Silk, J. B. (2013). The bystander effect in an N-person dictator game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds a strong bystander effect in an N-person dictator game: when there is only one dictator, recipients receive significantly more than when there are two or three dictators. The average transfer as a fraction of the 'equal share' drops from 57% (one dictator) to 36% (two or three dictators). Recipient payoffs are about 50% higher in the one-dictator condition than in the two- or three-dictator conditions. This effect persists even when uncertainty about others' actions is removed (strategy method) and when dictators can communicate and coordinate (bargaining method). The results suggest that most participants transform the situation into a prisoner's dilemma, leading to low transfers in groups. The paper does not study punishment or sanctions, but discusses the possibility that introducing punishment could alleviate the bystander effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the number of potential contributors in a many-to-one helping game (adjacent to PGG) reduces efficiency/welfare, even when communication or strategy methods are used. The absence of punishment or sanctions means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does establish a robust baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency declines with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar games without punishment, efficiency will be lower as group size increases. It also suggests that communication alone may not be sufficient to restore efficiency. However, since punishment is not present, the paper cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, only to set a control baseline.

  findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.

Since there is no punishment or sanctioning, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding LLMs' simulated social preferences or for comparison with human dictator game data, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Jimenez-Romero, C., & Page, K. M. (2022). Dying to cooperate: the role of environmental harshness in human collaboration. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The paper finds that environmental harshness is a key moderator of cooperative behavior in coordination games like the Stag Hunt. In both models and human experiments, individuals are more likely to choose cooperative, high-risk strategies when the environment is harsh and survival is threatened. Communication between agents (or players) allows for better coordination and increases the viability of cooperation. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms, and does not use a public goods game structure, but rather a coordination game with binary choices and pairwise matching. The main outcomes are behavioral (strategy choice and switching), with survival probability and energy reserves as related but not directly group efficiency outcomes.

It does, however, provide informative context about how environmental harshness and communication affect the viability of cooperation in coordination games. Since punishment and reward are not present, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the role of environmental context and communication in cooperation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Nockur, L., & Pfattheicher, S. (2021). The Beautiful Complexity of Human Prosociality: On the Interplay of Honesty-Humility, Intuition, and a Reward System. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reward system (the possibility of being rewarded by a third party) increases the likelihood and amount of giving in a dictator game. Honesty-Humility is also positively associated with giving. Intuitive decision making does not have a main effect, but among individuals low in Honesty-Humility, intuition reduces giving when no reward system is present. The reward system effect is robust across personality and decision-making style. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It does provide evidence that a reward system (not punishment) increases prosocial behavior in a dictator game, but the outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The design is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no analysis of efficiency or related payoff outcomes. Thus, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is very limited.

- source: Horiuchi, S. (2015). Emergence and Collapse of the Norm of Resource Sharing Around Locally Abundant Resources. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that workers' effort (reciprocity) increases with their own wage (direct reciprocity) and, when their own wage is unobservable, also with the coworker's wage (indirect reciprocity via signaling). When both wages are observable, the coworker's wage has no effect on effort, indicating that indirect reciprocity is driven by signaling about one's own treatment, not by altruism toward the coworker. There is no evidence that inequality aversion explains the observed patterns. The main outcome is effort, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent in that it studies reciprocity and information in a gift-exchange game, but the main outcome is effort, not efficiency, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings may inform predictions about the role of information and signaling in reciprocity, but not about punishment or efficiency. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency is limited.

- source: Dunning, D., Anderson, J. E., Schlösser, T., Ehlebracht, D., & Fetchenhauer, D. (2014). Trust at Zero Acquaintance: More a Matter of Respect Than Expectation of Reward. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that trust in zero-acquaintance trust games is driven more by internalized moral norms (especially a norm of respect) than by expectations of reward or risk tolerance. Participants report that they 'should' trust more than they 'want' to, and this sense of obligation predicts trust behavior. Making trust decisions public does not increase trust, suggesting the norm is moral rather than social. When the trust game is altered so that the recipient's character is not at stake (e.g., outcome determined by coin flip), trust rates drop. The paper does not study public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, and thus does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that in trust games without punishment, trust rates can be high due to internalized moral norms, even when efficiency or payoff maximization would not predict such behavior. The findings are only indirectly relevant to public goods games, and not at all relevant to punishment or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2010). People recognise when they are really anonymous in an economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, proposers' offers are sensitive to whether their decisions are public or anonymous, but not to the mere presence of others if anonymity is maintained. The presence of known others increases offers in public conditions but decreases them in anonymous-in-public conditions. Responders' minimum acceptable offers are not affected by anonymity or social context. The findings suggest that explicit information about anonymity can override social cues, and that reputation concerns drive higher offers when decisions are public. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and is not directly about public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is adjacent in relevance, as it studies the effect of anonymity and social context on cooperative behavior in the ultimatum game, but does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how social context and reputation concerns affect cooperative choices, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Rule Following Mitigates Collaborative Cheating and Facilitates the Spreading of Honesty Within Groups. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individuals who are more likely to follow arbitrary, costly rules are also more likely to behave honestly in situations where cheating is possible and profitable. In dyadic and four-person group die-rolling tasks, groups with more high rule-followers exhibit less collaborative cheating (i.e., lower rates of reporting doubles and lower average reported die-rolls). The presence of even a single high rule-follower in a group can reduce collaborative cheating among low rule-followers. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or any formal enforcement mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes beyond the behavioral proxies for dishonesty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related enforcement mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. Its findings are relevant to understanding the role of individual differences in rule-following and the dynamics of collaborative cheating in the absence of punishment, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Insko, C. A., Kirchner, J. L., Pinter, B., Efaw, J., & Wildschut, T. (2005). Interindividual-intergroup discontinuity as a function of trust and categorization: The paradox of expected cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that groups are less cooperative and more competitive than individuals in a one-shot, three-choice PDG-Alt game. Manipulating trust (high vs. low) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both groups and individuals, but does not significantly affect competition. Manipulating categorization (same vs. different) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both, but increases competition only for groups, especially among those high in guilt proneness. The main dependent variables are behavioral choices, not payoffs or efficiency. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how group context, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how group structure, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition in a PDG-variant. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline group vs. individual differences in cooperation in the absence of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on behavioral choices rather than payoffs limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency outcomes with or without punishment.

- source: Du, J., Thomas, M. G., Bårdsen, B. J., Mace, R., & Næss, M. W. (2019). Comparison of social complexity in two independent pastoralist societies. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both kinship and herding group membership predict gift-giving among pastoralists, with some cross-cultural differences: Saami herders in Norway emphasize reciprocity and group membership, while Tibetan herders in China emphasize kinship and giving to poorer herders. Gifts are more likely to be given within herding groups and to close kin, but the relative importance of these factors varies by site. There is no evidence of punishment, sanctions, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes. The association between gifts received and herd size (wealth) is negative in Tibetan sites (gifts go to poorer herders) and absent in Norwegian sites.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves allocation of resources in a social context, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some contextual insight into how cooperation is structured in real-world groups, but not in a way that informs the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Waring, T. M., & Bell, A. V. (2013). Ethnic dominance damages cooperation more than ethnic diversity: results from multi-ethnic field experiments in India. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing moderate irrational sentiment (either overestimating or underestimating one's own payoff) can increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest for moderate values of the irrationality parameter θ, with both positive (overconfidence) and negative (self-abasement) values promoting cooperation up to a point. Excessive irrationality in either direction reduces cooperation. The findings are based on simulations of strategy evolution on a lattice, with no explicit payoff or efficiency outcomes reported. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or payoff/efficiency outcomes. Its findings about irrational sentiment and cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma may be of limited, indirect relevance for understanding how non-standard updating rules affect cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Li, T., Li, H., & Zheng, J. D. (2024). Promoting cooperation in collective actions: Evidence from the reserve fund for maintenance in China. *International Review of Economics & Finance*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that requiring additional cash payments from homeowners (when the reserve fund is insufficient) increases the consent rate for maintenance projects. This effect is robust across OLS, IV, and Tobit models, and is stronger for projects proposed by homeowners' associations and for elevator maintenance. The mechanism appears to be increased salience and information provision, which motivates participation. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment/sanctions, and the outcome is the behavioral approval rate, not a payoff-based measure.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The findings are about how additional required payments (a form of cost salience or information) increase behavioral consent rates in a real-world collective action setting. While the context is adjacent to public goods games, the lack of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. It may be useful for understanding how cost salience or information affects participation, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Bluffstone, R., Dannenberg, A., Martinsson, P., Jha, P., & Bista, R. (2020). Cooperative behavior and common pool resources: Experimental evidence from community forest user groups in Nepal. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that Martu hunters who share a higher proportion of their foraging income are more central in cooperative hunting networks, suggesting that prosocial generosity (pecuniary distancing) is rewarded with more cooperative opportunities. High producers do not keep or receive more for themselves, and the best hunters often keep less than others. The benefits of generosity are indirect, via increased access to cooperation, not direct material gain. No evidence is provided on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency outcomes. Its findings on prosocial signaling and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation in the absence of formal enforcement, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency. The game structure is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no manipulation of punishment or efficiency.

  findings: The meta-analyses find that artificial surveillance cues do not significantly increase generosity, measured either as the amount given or the probability of giving. The effect sizes are very small and not significantly different from zero. The studies included cover a range of economic games and donation paradigms, but the dependent variable is always generosity, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no analysis of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency-related outcomes. The findings suggest that artificial surveillance cues should not be expected to increase prosocial giving in anonymous settings.

The meta-analyses focus on the effect of artificial surveillance cues on generosity, not on punishment, sanctions, or efficiency. While one included study uses a public goods game, the outcome is individual generosity, not group efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games.

  findings: The study finds that in sociable weavers, individual investment in cooperative nest construction (measured as time spent and items added) is positively predicted by genetic relatedness to the group. Males invest more than females, and time of day also affects investment. No evidence is provided about punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. - source: Ember, C. R., Skoggard, I., Ringen, E. J., & Farrer, M. (2018). Our better nature: Does resource stress predict beyond-household sharing?. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that beyond-household food and labor sharing is nearly universal in nonindustrial societies. Societies experiencing higher resource stress (e.g., famine, natural hazards, chronic scarcity) are more likely to have frequent and extensive sharing customs, especially seasonal sharing. However, increased resource stress does not predict broader social or geographic scope of sharing (i.e., with non-relatives or beyond the community); in some cases, sharing with non-relatives is less common under high resource stress. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve experimental manipulation or public goods game paradigms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve experimental games, punishment, or payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides contextual evidence that cooperation (in the form of sharing) is more frequent in environments with higher resource stress, suggesting that environmental risk can increase cooperative customs. This may be indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in environments with or without punishment, but does not inform the effect of punishment or game design dimensions on efficiency.

- source: Swakman, V., Molleman, L., Ule, A., & Egas, M. (2016). Reputation-based cooperation: empirical evidence for behavioral strategies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that people condition their helping decisions on both first-order (recipient's past helping) and second-order (context of recipient's past helping/defection) information. Donors are especially interested in 2nd-order information about defection, and tend to reward 'justified defection' (denying help to defectors). There is strong individual heterogeneity: some use only first-order information, some use both, and a minority are unconditional cooperators or defectors. The availability of 2nd-order information (free or costly) does not significantly increase overall helping rates compared to the control, though helping rates are numerically higher when 2nd-order information is available. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The game is an iterated indirect helping game with binary helping decisions, and the main outcomes are behavioral (helping rates, information use, strategy classification). While the findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms of reputation-based cooperation, they do not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The only indirect relevance is that richer information about others' behavior can (non-significantly) increase helping rates, but this is not about efficiency or payoff, nor about punishment.

- source: Molho, C., Soraperra, I., Schulz, J. F., & Shalvi, S. (2025). Guilt drives prosociality across 20 countries. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 20 countries, participants were more likely to make prosocial choices in dictator games when they had full information about the consequences of their actions (guilt condition) compared to when they could remain ignorant. Making choices observable to others (shame condition) had negligible or inconsistent effects on prosociality. Individual-level guilt-proneness predicted greater responsiveness to information, but country-level differences were not robust moderators. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or group interaction, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings on the effect of information and observability on individual prosocial choice in dictator games may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms of prosociality, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Haley, K. J., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2005). Nobody's watching? Subtle cues affect generosity in an anonymous economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that subtle visual cues of being watched (eyespots) significantly increase generosity in a one-shot dictator game, as measured by both the mean amount allocated and the proportion of dictators who give any money. The effect of reducing auditory cues (earmuffs) is small and not statistically significant. The results suggest that even in anonymous, noniterated economic games, intuitive or affective cues of observability can increase prosocial behavior. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Its findings about subtle cues of observability increasing generosity in dictator games may be contextually interesting for understanding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., & Schulhofer-Wohl, J. (2022). The impact of distance on parochial altruism: An experimental investigation. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The experiment finds that parochial altruism (allocation to the between-group account, which benefits the in-group and harms the out-group) is higher when both in-group and out-group distances are high. Low distance to the out-group reduces both altruism and parochial altruism. The main behavioral outcomes are the propensity and amount allocated to within-group and between-group accounts. There is no analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are robust to alternative distance measures and controls for group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The behavioral outcomes (parochial altruism, altruism) are not directly transferable to payoff-based efficiency predictions. The game is an intergroup conflict variant (IPD-MD), not a standard PGG, and the main manipulations concern group distance, not institutional design features like punishment or reward. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2019). Collective Action for War and for Peace: A Case Study among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that collective action for war is often easier to mobilize than for peace, due to emotional triggers, private benefits for some individuals (especially young men), and the contagiousness of anger and revenge. Peacemaking, by contrast, requires overcoming internal rifts, mistrust, and the lack of unifying emotions, but is facilitated by strong leadership, compensation payments, and the involvement of broader social networks (including women and affinal kin). The development of rules and institutions for peacemaking (e.g., compensation, mediation) reduced the costs and destructiveness of war, promoted social complexity, and shifted incentives toward peace. However, the breakdown of traditional norms (e.g., with the introduction of guns) led to more destructive wars and greater reluctance to participate. Sanctions against free riders in war are rare due to the importance of brotherhood, but in peacemaking, non-contributors can be gradually reintegrated. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in a formal sense, but describes the social and material costs and benefits of war and peace.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It offers contextual and theoretical insights into how collective action, norm enforcement, and compensation (a form of sanction) operate in a real-world society, and how these mechanisms affect the costs and benefits of group action. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in a way that can be mapped to PGG outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in illustrating the complexity of sanctioning, the importance of leadership and social structure, and the potential for compensation to restore balance after conflict. It should be used as contextual background rather than as direct input for quantitative prediction.

- source: Alvarez-Benjumea, A., & Valentim, V. (2024). The Enforcement of Political Norms. *British Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that individuals in Spain perceive public displays of support for the radical-right party Vox as less socially and morally appropriate and more harmful than support for other parties. Respondents are more likely to view sanctioning (especially indirect sanctions like gossip and avoidance) as appropriate and report greater willingness to engage in such sanctions against radical-right supporters. The willingness to sanction is not strongly predicted by political ideology or interest, but is correlated with age, gender, education, local support for Vox, and emotional reactions (anger, disgust). The study does not measure or discuss any payoff-based or efficiency outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or similar economic game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it offers detailed insights into the social and psychological mechanisms of norm enforcement and sanctioning in a political context, it does not use a game-theoretic or economic game design, nor does it report any group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG or similar environments.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Aksoy, O. (2021). Does Herding Undermine the Trust Enhancing Effect of Reputation? An Empirical Investigation with Online-Auction Data. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: Using a large dataset of eBay auctions, the study shows that sellers with more positive reputations are more likely to sell their items and at higher prices, while negative reputations reduce these outcomes. Buyers exhibit herding behavior, being more likely to bid on items that already have bids, but this effect peaks and then declines as the number of bidders increases. Importantly, even when herding, buyers still consider seller reputation, and the effect of reputation on sales and price remains significant. There is no evidence that herding undermines the efficiency or trust-enhancing effect of reputation systems in this market context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation mechanisms (a form of informal sanctioning) increase efficiency in online market environments by raising both the probability of sale and the price sellers can obtain. While not a public goods game or direct punishment setting, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of reputation-based sanctions on efficiency in environments where trust and cooperation are important. The results suggest that, even in the presence of herding (social influence), reputation systems continue to enhance efficiency. However, the market context differs from standard PGGs, so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

- source: Gächter, S., Molleman, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2025). Why people follow rules. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that 55-70% of participants follow an arbitrary costly rule even when acting anonymously and alone, and when rule-breaking has no consequences for others. Social expectations and intrinsic respect for rules explain most rule-following. Adding externalities (harm to a charity) or punishment (with varying probabilities) increases rule-following rates, but a substantial baseline of rule-following exists without these. Observing peer rule-breaking reduces rule-following, but even with all peers breaking the rule, a significant fraction still comply. The main outcome is behavioral (rule-following), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. While it provides evidence that punishment increases rule-following in a single-player, non-strategic, asocial rule-following task, the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the context is not a PGG or its variant. The findings may be relevant for understanding the motivational basis of compliance and the effect of sanctions on rule-following, but cannot be directly transferred to efficiency predictions in PGG-like environments.

- source: Berger, J. (2024). Social capital is associated with cooperation and indirect norm enforcement in the field: behavioural evidence from Switzerland. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that neighborhoods with higher social capital exhibit higher rates of cooperation (as measured by the lost-letter technique) and more frequent indirect norm enforcement (as measured by bystanders picking up litter after a staged transgression). Direct enforcement (verbal confrontation) is rare and does not differ significantly between neighborhoods. Residents in high social capital neighborhoods have higher empirical and normative expectations of norm compliance and lower fear of retaliation. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any payoff-based group interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about real-world cooperation and informal norm enforcement, not about payoff, efficiency, or sanctioning in economic games. The norm enforcement studied is indirect (e.g., picking up litter) and not costly punishment as in PGGs. Therefore, while the paper may be contextually relevant for understanding the social foundations of cooperation and informal enforcement, it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2009). Experimental Games and Games of Life among the Ju/'hoan Bushmen. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The Ju/'hoan Bushmen made low offers and rarely punished in anonymous dictator and ultimatum games, behaving more selfishly than in their daily lives, where sharing and social sanctions are common. The anonymity and lack of social context in the experimental games removed the cultural institutions that normally support generosity and punishment. In daily life, sharing is widespread and norm violations are sanctioned, usually through verbal criticism and exclusion rather than costly punishment. The study highlights the importance of cultural context and institutions for the expression of other-regarding behavior and social sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a public goods game or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the importance of social context and cultural institutions for cooperation and punishment, but do not inform quantitative predictions about punishment's effect on efficiency in experimental economic games with or without punishment. The study suggests that the presence or absence of social context and identification may strongly moderate the willingness to punish or cooperate, but provides no direct evidence for the target prediction task.

- source: Mazar, N., Amir, O., & Ariely, D. (2008). The Dishonesty of Honest People: A Theory of Self-Concept Maintenance. *Journal of Marketing Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across six lab experiments, the authors find that (1) people cheat when given the opportunity, but only by a limited amount; (2) reminders of moral standards (e.g., Ten Commandments, honor code) eliminate or reduce cheating; (3) increasing categorization malleability (e.g., using tokens instead of money) increases cheating; (4) cheating is not sensitive to the probability of being caught or the magnitude of external rewards within the tested range; (5) cheating does not affect self-reported self-concept as honest/moral; and (6) beliefs about others' cheating do not affect individual cheating. The results support a psychological model of dishonesty based on self-concept maintenance, not external punishment or cost-benefit analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or public goods outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not manipulate or analyze punishment or sanctions as a treatment, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding psychological mechanisms of norm compliance and dishonesty, and may be indirectly informative for the design of interventions (e.g., moral reminders) to reduce cheating, but do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Schläpfer, A. (2025). The social benefits of anti-social punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study documents that cultural background predicts the likelihood and speed of retaliation after a foul in professional soccer. Retaliation by the victim reduces the probability of repeated fouls by the perpetrator, but only if the perpetrator is from a culture that values revenge. Formal sanctions (yellow cards) reduce both the likelihood of retaliation and repeated offenses, and are about three times as effective as informal retaliation in deterring repeated fouls. There is evidence of crowding out: when a foul is formally sanctioned, victims are less likely to retaliate. The study does not report or analyze any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that informal and formal sanctions can reduce repeated transgressions in a real-world setting, and that the effectiveness of informal sanctions depends on cultural context. However, because the setting is not a public goods game or a close variant, and because no payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, the paper offers only indirect, contextual support for the prediction task. It may inform mechanism discussions about the interaction of formal and informal punishment, and about heterogeneity in punishment effectiveness, but does not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gould, R. V. (2000). Revenge as sanction and solidarity display: An analysis of vendettas in nineteenth-century Corsica. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that only about 10-15% of violent attacks were followed by revenge, and that such retaliation was most likely when the original attack involved multiple assailants (collaboration) or targeted nondisputants (kin uninvolved in the dispute). Revenge was not indiscriminately directed at all kin of the offender, but rather at those who had demonstrated solidarity with the original offender, either by collaborating in the attack or by aiding the offender afterward. The structure of revenge mirrored the kin relationships involved in the original offense, suggesting a strategic, solidarity-signaling function. The findings challenge the idea of automatic collective responsibility and instead support a model of 'contingent collectivism,' where group-level retaliation is conditional on group-level aggression.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about the social logic and structure of revenge in a real-world, stateless society context, not about experimental games or efficiency. However, it may offer indirect, contextual insight into the conditions under which group-level sanctions (punishment) are likely to be deployed: namely, when group-level (collaborative) violations occur. For the prediction task, this suggests that the presence of group-level aggression or collaboration may be a moderator of the likelihood or intensity of punishment, but the paper does not address efficiency or payoff consequences.

- source: Kasper, C., & Mulder, M. B. (2015). Who Helps and Why? Cooperative Networks in Mpimbwe. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that in a rural Tanzanian village, helping behavior is generally low and is best predicted by reciprocity (contingency), with kinship and affinal relationships also playing roles. Wealthier individuals are less likely to participate in reciprocal helping relationships and receive less help. Geographic proximity and age also influence helping patterns. The study discusses the erosion of traditional cooperation networks in the context of increasing market integration and inequality. Witchcraft accusations are mentioned as a form of social sanction, but there is no formal punishment system or experimental manipulation of sanctions. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or any formal game, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. The study is observational, not experimental, and focuses on naturalistic helping behavior, not on the effects of introducing or varying punishment mechanisms. Witchcraft accusations are discussed as a form of social pressure, but not in a way that is structurally or functionally equivalent to PGG punishment treatments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some adjacent context about the role of social sanctions in real-world cooperation, but not in a way that is mappable to the target prediction problem.

- source: O'Gorman, R., Wilson, D. S., & Miller, R. R. (2008). An evolved cognitive bias for social norms. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Across three experiments, participants showed significantly better recall for normative social information compared to nonnormative information, regardless of sex or perceived importance of the information. The effect was robust to changes in recall format (multiple choice vs. cued recall), instructional framing, and participant nationality (US vs. UK). The findings support the hypothesis of an evolved cognitive bias for social norms, but do not address economic game behavior, efficiency, or the effects of punishment on cooperation or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report or analyze any game design dimensions, group payoffs, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are limited to cognitive recall of normative information and do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Berger, R., Rauhut, H., Prade, S., & Heibing, D. (2012). Bargaining over waiting time in ultimatum game experiments. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, in an ultimatum game over waiting time (a loss), proposers typically offer to split the waiting time equally, mirroring results from monetary ultimatum games. Anonymity (between subjects and/or experimenter) does not significantly affect the distribution of offers. Only one offer was rejected across all treatments. Opportunity cost (measured by outside temperature) and gender do not significantly affect offers. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, and does not involve punishment or public goods game structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. Its findings on anonymity and loss framing in ultimatum games may be of tangential interest for understanding social preferences, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Kardas, M., Shaw, A., & Caruso, E. M. (2018). How to Give Away Your Cake and Eat It Too: Relinquishing Control Prompts Reciprocal Generosity. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that when one person abdicates a resource allocation decision to another, the recipient is more likely to respond generously (give the better resource to the abdicator) than in a baseline where no abdication occurs. This effect is robust across hypothetical and real-stakes settings, and is mediated in part by perceptions of the abdicator's generosity. The studies are all dyadic, with two individuals and a binary allocation (better/worse resource), and do not involve repeated interaction, group cooperation, or any form of punishment or sanction. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design, outcomes, and mechanisms studied (abdication, reciprocity, perceived generosity) are not related to punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes in group cooperation games. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game-like settings.

- source: Arbia, A., & Carbonnier, G. (2016). Human nature and development aid: IR and the biology of altruism. *Journal of International Relations and Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that ODA as a share of GNI is persistent over time and increases with donor country wealth and government spending, supporting the idea that altruism may be partially hardwired. There is mixed evidence for the influence of cultural or institutional factors (nurture), such as income inequality and political competition. The analysis is at the country-policy level and does not involve experimental games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report on game design dimensions, control or treatment efficiency, or any related laboratory or field experiments. Its findings on macro-level ODA policy and the nature of altruism are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in public-goods-game-like settings.

- source: Cai, W., Huang, X. Q., Wu, S., & Kou, Y. (2015). Dishonest behavior is not affected by an image of watching eyes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that implicit reputation cues (images of watching eyes) do not reduce dishonest behavior in laboratory settings, even when dishonesty leads to higher monetary payoffs or reputational benefits. This null effect was consistent across different tasks and motivational framings. The study also finds that higher moral identity predicts less dishonesty, but the eyes image does not affect this. The pilot experiment replicates the known effect of eyes images on generosity in a dictator game, but the main experiments focus on dishonesty, not cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. The findings about implicit reputation cues and dishonesty may be of tangential interest for understanding the limits of non-punitive interventions, but do not inform the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.
